In continuing their discussions begun in November
(Political Report for November), the American Ambassedor
held on Decerber 4 at the Foreign Office a further con-
versation on Americen-Jepanese relations with the For-
eign Minister at the latter's request. This conversa-
tion, it was later explained to the Embassy, was design-
ed to clarify the Japanese position, specially as regards
negative measures being taken by the Japanese Government
for the improvement of American-Japanese relations, in
the light of the Anbassador's suggestion made in his in-
terview with the Foreign :'inister on November 4 regard-
ing the importance of Japan's presenting evidence that
an end would be put to the acts of the Japanese in China
committed against Americans and American rights and in-
terests. Admiral Nomura first categorically stated in
this conversation that the impression, which he realized
persists in the United States that the ects on the part
of the Japanese were doliberate and that Japan, moreovor,
intended to expel American interests from China, was con-
trary to fact. He then reiterated the assurance that the
incidents which had occurred werc accidontal and he added
that stringent orders had been issued and full arrange-
ments made for the protection and respect of American prop-
erty and citizens in China, He averred, furthermore, that
those limitations which have been imposed on commeroial
activities in China resulted from military operations but
were

-2-
were exceptional and temporary and would be removed when
peace comes. He continued by saying thet he felt that,
responively to the desire of the American Government to
improve relations, the Japanese Govcrnment would be able
to adopt certain positive measures in that connection
which it then had under study. He further stated that
he hoped that through the efforts of the American Em-
bassy and the Foreign Office it would be possible to
deal with and bring to a quick solution the pending ques-
tions*
On December 6 the Director of the A..erican Bureau of
the Foreign Office informed the Counselor of the Embassy
of an outline of the views of the Japanese Government
with regard to an approach towards the adjustment of
cases and problems involving American rights and inter-
ests in China. The position of the Japanese Government
was given as one of considering that theso could be divi-
ded into throe categories:
1. Cases involving direct injury by Japanese to
Anerican lives and proporty.
2. Politico-cconomic casos or those which could be
described as involving restriction or prevention of the
exercise of A:.erican rights, and
3. Basic principles involving conflicts of policy
and objociives, such as the territorial and administra-
tive integrity of Cnina.

The Rice Situation.
The adverse situation as regerds the price and supply
of rico, which had appeared during October cnd November
(Political Roport for Novenber) took on serious propor-
tions during December. Public feeling in the matter be-
came exacerbated, with open criticism leveled cgainst the
Government for its having allowed the matter to reach a
point

shortage of this prime national food. with the agricul-

to the next year's crop, beginning November 1, 1940. The
questionsof mal-distribution of rice in the cities and in.
dustrial centers and of hoarding by the farmers and mer-
chants who were refusing to relcose or sell tho comnodity

wes invoked, but it was proviled that the enforcement of

o, entitled "Report of Conditions in Japan

Effective February 1, 1940, there has been
promulgated an ordinance providing for Govern-
ment control of marine transportation. It is
based on the National Mobilization Law and
supplants the Provisional Shipping Control Law
(page 26).
Currenoy in ciroulation at the close of
January 1940 is reported to have been
Y3,277,000,000, an 1ncrease of Y1,122,000,000
over January 31, 1939. There is usually a
large contraction of currency circulation in
the first month after the close of the calendar
year, but this year the January decline in
circulation as compared with December was only
Y116,000,000(fag27).

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a)
Termination of the Treaty of Commerce
and Navigation or 1911.
The interview on January 23 of the Japanese Am-
bassaior at Washington with Mr. Berle, when the ques-
川
tion of the commercial relations between the United
States and Japan after the expiration on January 26
of the Treaty of Commerce and Navigation was discussed,*
was prominently written up in the Japanese press. The
articles in this regard were headed by a statement to
the effect that "the developments" in the Far East re-
ferred to by Mr. Berle meant Japan's attitude towards
the rights and interests of Americans in China.
On January 25 an Imperial Ordinance was promul-
gated by the Japanese Government providing for the ex-
tension, for the time being, of the benefits of low
tariff conventions to American goods after the expira-
tion of the treaty. This measure, it was reported in
the press, was taken on the part of the Japanese Gov-
ernment in response to the American decision not to
impose for the present discriminating duties upon Jap-
anese goods and thereby to effect a continuance of the
present basis of commercial relations between the two
countries, despite the lapse of the treaty. It was
also understood that by administrative procedure the
entry and residence of American citizens would be regu-
lated in a manner corresponding procisoly with rogula-
tions in that respect enforced by the Unitad States.**

II.
RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
1. Stopping of the S.S. Asama Maru and Removal
from It of German Seamen by British Cruiser.
On January 21 the Japanese trans-Pacific passenger
ship Asama Maru of the N.Y.Y. line was stopped by a
British cruiser less than loo miles out from Yokohama and
was boarded by a party of British officers and sailors.
From a list produced by a British officer, twenty-one of
a number of some fifty odd German seamen who were return-
ing by way of Japan to Germany were called out and removed
from the Jananese ship. The following day the British Eiu-
bassy issued an announcement to the effect that the Brit-
ish had a right, in accordance with international law, to
remove from a neutral vessel all Germans of military age
as being reservists in the German armed forces but that,
in this instance, only those seamen who were regarded as
technicians and skilled ratings and, therefore, of special
value to German military operations, were removed.*
News of this incident brought forth in Japan an im-
mediate display of resentment, denunciation of Great Brit-
ain, and demand for strong action to be taken by the Japa-
nese Government in the matter. The Foreign Office issued
a stetement that the Japanese authorities regarded the
forceful removal of the twenty-one German seamen from the
Asama Maru as "very disagreeable". The view generally taken
in Japan wes that the act was illogal, but the agitation in
the
*Embassy's telegram no. 37, January 22, 6 p.m.

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
the matter centered particularly around tho point that
the stopping of the Asama Maru represented an insult to
and offend d the prestige of Japan, especially in that
it occurred so close to Japan "in the sphere of Japanose
influonce" cnd, as it was put, "within the very view of
the sacred Mt. Fuji". It is also to be noted, as was
explained by a member of the Foreign Ofrice, that the
commotion aroused over this incidont could be consid-
ered as partly attributable to the tonse norvous state
of the Jepanose people, in view of the difficuit nrob-
lems that Janan was facing in its intornal and foreign
affairs. On Jenuary 22, the British Ambessedor was

by the Vice-llinister, strongly protesting ageinst the
romoval of thc seamen. This note, following along the
lines of the statement issucd by the Foreign Office,
stressed the unfriendly nature of the act, referred to
the fact that the seizure took place "in waters near
Jepan", set forth tho position of the Japaneso Govern-
ment that arrest of nationals of belligerent countries
on neutral vessels by forces of another bolligerent coun-
try must bo "limited to those actually ombodied in the
armed forces" and, after reserving in advance the right to

the British Governmont pey serious hoed to the end that

By

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
-30-
By the end of the month the problem of power supply
still remained in a critical state.*
The question arises whether the shortage in the power
supply can be attributed entirely or largely to the China
conflict. One fact is perfectly clear, and that is the in-
crease in Japan's industrial capacity which has taken place
during the past few years has run ahead of the power industry.
It might be argued that the concomitants of the conflict --
the diversion of the large part of the industrial capacity
of the nation to munitions and other non-productive indus-
tries and the over-rapid expansion of war industries -- have
brought about the power crisis. On the other hand, no one
seems to know for certain what portion of the increase in in-
dustrial capacity represents an expansion of war industries
and what portion is made up of the expansion in productive in-
dustries. The basic cause, in any event, appears to be the
failure to integrate the output of power -- including, of
course, adequate coal production -- with industrial capacity.
Comparatively few important official measures relat-
ing to industries, finance and trade were introduced during
January. The most important measure was the Raw Silk Dis-
tribution Control Law, providing for the regulation of the
prices of silk for domestic use and for the rcgulation of the
supply of silk for oxport and for domestic consumption. A
specific amount is to be allotted for domestic consumption,
a card system is to be applicd in the distribution of silk
to manufacturers, and a reduction is to be made in the num-
ber of the various kinds of silk textiles produced.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) General Relations.
The month of Felruary was marked by no new outstand-
711.94
ing developments in Japanese-A" rican relations, but in
Japan, through the Dict session and through the press, a
vast amount of concern was manifested over the current
situation and future eventualities in the relations be-
tween the two countries.
In the Diet, insistent demands were made for an enun-
ciation and clarification of the Government's policies and
stand on the general and specific points at issue with the
United States, primarily as they related to Cnina. Iu re-
ply to one such interpellation the Fureign Minister stated
on Fubruary 2 that the establishment of close economic
ties between Juyan, "Manchukuo", and Cuina does not mean
the oustine of economic activities of foreien countries;
rather, on the contrary, there will remain tremendously
broad fields of economic expansion for tlose countries.
Tose countries, he added, appear to fail to understand
this by confusing the effects of military operations and
strategical considerations on their rights and interests
in Caina with Japan's policy in regard to the "row order
in Eust Asia".
Op the same occasion he reiterated t'e pronouncement
made by the Government in Ja.:uary that the Government would
continue to make ever" effort to arrange for a new trade
treaty wit!: the United States, stating, however, that it
was felt that this would require considerable time. In
then referring to the uneasiness in Japu.. over the treaty-
less situation he stated that the "uneasiness" eed not be
asgravated and that it was, in that connection, of utmost
importance

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
importance that Japan and the United States deal with
the situation calmly and circumspectly.*
O February 7th, in reply to a demand made that
Japan's announcement to reopen the Yuugtze should be
immediately withdrawn since it had been made, but with-
out success, to conciliate the Uited States, Mr. Arita
stated that the opening of the Yangtze was not made on
a bargaining basis as a factor in the negotiations for
a new commercial treaty and that, furthermore, since
the announcement was made unilaterally, Japan had no
obligation whatever to open the River.**
T.e matter of denunciation of the Nine-Power Trea-
ty "will require still further consideration", the For-
eign Minister is reported to have stated on the same day.
This reply was made in response to a statement by a Diet
member that the Treaty should be denounced on the grounds
that conditions had changed and that that treaty was the
Versailles Treaty of the F:i Esst by which other nations
sought to bind Jupan.*** On Feoruary 9, Mr. Arita stated
that the Treaty was unsuited to the present situation but
professed that it was not certain whether its abolition
would hasten or rinder the establishment of a "ew order
in East Asia". Or: Fubruary 28, again referrine to the
Nine-Power Treaty, he declared that, contrary to the
contention of the U..ited States, "Japan does not admit
that it has ignored the Treaty, and that if an attempt
is made by force of arms to compel Japan to recognize
this contention, a real problem would arise"(Sio).****

II. RELATIONS VITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
Great Britain.
ASAMA MARU incident.
It will be recalled that at the end of January nego-
tiations were still being carried on between the Foreign
Office and the British Embassy with regard to the sclution
of the incident rising out of the stopping of the ASAMA
MARU by a British cruiser and the removal therefrom of
twenty-one Germen seamen. Following the exchange of
several further communications between the two governments
the incident was settled on February 5. Both countries
continued officially to maintain their positions--the
Japanese Government contending that the British action was
not justifiable in international law, referring in support
of its position to the Declaration of London, the TRENT
case, et cetera, and holding to its formal request for the
return of all of the twenty-one Germans; and the British
Government maintaining its stand that it was "fully en-
titled under international law to remove the German offi-
cers and ratings"--but the British "as an earnest of their
desire to reach an amicable settlement of the case" agreed
to release to the Japanese nine of the men seized from the
ASAMA MARU, and the Japanese for their part undertook not
to embark on Japanese vessels citizens of a belligerent
nation who are embodied in its armed forces or suspected
to be so.*
2.
*Embassy's telegrams no. 77, February 3, 2 p.m.;

2. Representations made by the British Embassy
during February, and replies to previous
representations.
On February 2l thc British Embassy in a memorandum

the bombing by Japanese aircraft of the Haiphong-Yunnan

On February 10 the British Embassy addressed a note
along lines similar to those of the American representa-
tions in regard to the imposition by the local Japanese-
sponsored authorities at Swatow of taxes on imports and
exports.**
In a note dated January 17, 1940, the Foreign Office
replied to the British representations of December ll,
1939, concerning the continued closure to foreign shipping
other than Japanese of the port of Swatow. In this reply
it was stated that the Japanese authorities consider for
reasons of strategic necessity that restrictions must
still be imposed upon the passage to and from Swatow of
persons, goods, and shipping; that the Japanese had
permitted certain firms of their own nationality to handle
trade in necessary goods at the port which, because of the
existence of extraterritorial rights which would have pre-
vented strict supervision thereof, could not be entrusted
to third-power companies; but that the Japanese Govern-
ment intended to reopen the harbor as soon as improvement
in conditions so warranted.***

(b) China.
1. Military operations.*
It will be recalled that at the end of January the
Japanese were engaged in a large-scale drive wastward
from Paotow in Suiyuan Province. During the firs cays
of February the advance was vigorously pushed, and on
February 3, Wuyuan was captured, followed by ths occupa-
tion of Linho on the next day, and Shanpa on February 5.
In an eight- to nine-day period Japanese forces had ad-
and
vanced some hundred fifty miles and had gone as far west
as the border of Ninghsia Province. It was announced
later in the month that on February 15 Japanese forces
were withdrawn from the entire Wuyuan area for reasons
that were not disclosed in this country.
In South China, the Japanese offensive launched at
the end of January, reportedly to meet a counter-offensive
of a large number of Chinese troops on Nanning, was
brought during the first days of February, according to
Japanese accounts, to a successful conclusion. In the
Nanning-Pinyang-Yuming area, by February 8, the Japanese
claimed to have completely routed the Chinese troops after
severe fighting. Like the withdrawal in the Suiyuan cam-
paign, however, after that date the Japanese forces were
reported to have retired to their positions held previous
to the campaign just completed. Whether the withdrawal
was deliberate or forced by Chinese pressure could not
be ascertained.
Other

heavy and indecisive fighting near Yuhu in Anhwei Province
against a fairly large-scale Chinese offensive aimed at

Almost daily publicity was given in Japan, with murked
flourish, to the alleged dissension and fighting between
the Communist forces and the adherents of the KuomintanE.
Reports to this effect asserted that several severe battles
on a large scale had occurred between the two factions.
It was intimated that there might be a disruption of Chinese
resistance to the Japanese through these internecine con-
flicts. Despite the seriousness of the dissension, however,
no tangible signs were to be noted of any diminution of the
Chinese opposition to the Japanese military force.

2. General relations.
Much comment appeared in Japan during the month
under review concerning the establishment of a proposed
new central régime for China. It was reported that
arrangements were steadily maturing for the inauguration
at an early date of the régime with its capital at Nanking.
According to the reports, the régime would be headed by a
central political council under the leadership of ang
Ching-wei, and the Eovernment under this council would
absorb the Nanking Renovation Government &nd the Peiping
régime. A large amount of eutonomy would be éiven to the
North China area while the Inner Mongolian area would be
recognized as an almost entirely autonomous unit wherein
Japan would have special interest. No official announce-
ment wea made concerning the contents of the peace terms
agreed upon between  an Chine-wei and the Japanese Govern-
ment at the end of December last year, although they were
reported to heve been revealec to the Liet in a secret
session on February 15. It is to be noted, however, that
two deserters from the Tane group published in Fong Kong
on January 18 what purported to be a Chinese version of
the agreement, and which in effect gave Japan what amounted
to almost complete control and domination over most of the
occupied arees of Cri.a.* The terms set forth in that
version were denled as being correct by ang Ching-wei;

(c) France
1. Bombing of the Haiphong-Yunnan Railway.
Continuing the raids conducted in the latter part
of last year and during January, the Japanese air forces
again bombed on several occasions during February the
Haiphong-Yunnan Reilway. During the air raid that
occurred on February 1 a train was hit and more than
fifty persons, including five French, were killed.
In this ara three subsequent raids Japanese planes
were reported to have flown over Indo-China, and on
one occasion to have dropped bombs in French Territory.
The French mbassy lodged strong protests with the
Foreign Office concerning these incidents.
A

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
A rosponse to his representations received by
the French Ambassador on February 5 from the Foreign
Minister stated in substance that so long as the
reilway continued to be a transit route of arms supply
the attacks on it would be continued, and it was
added that the cessation of supplies to the Chungking
Government over the reilway, to be fulfilled by the
French before the Japanese attacks would be stopped,
should anply not only to arms end ammunition but
besides to other articles which the French Government
itself had prchibited in time of war. The Japanese
Government contended that even though no declaretion
of war had been made, large-scale hostilities have
taken place in China ano therefore France should
recognize the "reality' in dealing with the situation.

2. Franco-Japanese Trade Agreement.
Some publicity was given in the press during the month
under review to the question of renewal of the Franco-
Japanese trade agreement vhich was to expire on March 15.
It was held that difficulvies might be expectec in this
regard because France needs munitions and othel commodities
required for military purposes, such as calcium, industrial
salts, and iron ore, which had made up a large part of
French and Indo-Chinese exports to Japan, because France
in its war-time eoonomy was limiting importation of many
commodities offered by this country, and because un-
favorable reaction in France to the bombing of the Yunnan
railway was militating against the conclusion of an agree-
ment with this country. It is to be noted that the press
spoke in laudatory terms of France's having strictly lived
up to the present agreement, having even after the outbreak
of the European war "allowed exports of munitions to Japan
to continue."

Argentina
There arrived in Japan on February 16 an Argentine
economic mission, compcsed of six delegates, invited to
this courtry by the Japan Chamber of Commerce for the
purpose of exploring the crounds for increased trede
between the two countries. Japan's total forcien trade
with tne latter country in 19s7 reached a hirh point of
almost eighty-five million yen but subsequently dropped to
less than twenty million yen last year, due in some part to
restrictions imposed by the Argentine Government on Japan's
exports to the Argentine. It was hoped that through the
negotiations it will be possible to build up again the trade.
In the discussions which took place during the month there
was stressed on the part of Japan desire to supply Argentina
with large quantities of cotton textiles, pottery, and
miscellaneous goods, in return for which Japan would take
wool, casein, canned meats, cereals, hides and skins,
quebracho, raw cotton, et cetera.*

improvement of trade between the two countries which amounted
to some forty-four million yen in l937 but decreased to less

be in the nature of a "compensation treaty" providing in
part for barter and in part for free trade between the two
countries. Japan hopes to exchange silk, tea, bamboo,
camphor, and miscellaneous items, for copper, pig iron,
potash, mercury, cork, salt, anc olive oil. Japan's trade
with Spain in the last few years has dwindled almost to
the vanishing point. Even during normal times it has not
been large, imports and exports amountin in 1935 to only
eight million yen.

(i) General International Relations
The Foreign Minister reiterated before the Diet on
several occasions during the month under review thet Japan
intended to maintain its position of following a policy of
"autonomous diplomacy." He declared on February 7 that it
was the intention of the Government to continue to adhere
to the anti-Comintern pact in the future, despite the fact
that the German-Soviet agreement was considered by the
Government as not being compatible with the spirit of the
paot, but that there was no plan contemplated to strengthen it.

Subsequent sessions of the Diet during February were
devoted to debates on Government policies and bills pre-

these debates.
The outstanding event in the Diet proceedings was an
interpellation on February 2 by Mr. Takao Saito, a member
of the Minseito, the largest political party at present,
who delivered a forceful end searching attack on the
Government's China policy. Much of the speech was expunged
from the records and publication thereof was forbidden.

(b) Official price control end reguletion
of nationel economy.
The important and pressing internal problem of prices
and economic adjustments came up for a great deal of dis-
cussion during Februnry. Interpellators in the Diet raised
the questions thet heve been under heated public discussion
for the past six montus and more, concerning the supply of
daily necessiti:s and the expansion of productive capacity.
It was held that militating against these two needs were
the current officially fixed low prices which, because they
were artifically maintained at too low levels, were being
flagrantly evaded. Thet evasion in turn resulted in
profiteering, maladjustment in the distribution of com-
modities, and othor unfavorable economic effects. Because
of

even vero forced to reduce their output. The essence
of the Government's policy, es brought out in the re-

continue to adhere to a low-price policy, but to adjust
the rates set et the prevailing levels of September 18,

VI. ECONOMIC AL FII.ICIAL CONLITIONS
Japanese commercial, financial, and industrial attention
was centered during the month under review on the proceedings
of the Diet for an indication of the trend of future economic
plans of the Government, end pendin clarifiction of such
plans business generally remained static. tock exchange
transactions reflected the cautious attitude on the part of
traders and investors, with volume and value of transactions
declining sharply from the level of the previous month.
Moreover, the prospects of more intensive official regulation
of industry and the prob&bility of further restrictions on
dividands had a depressing effect on share values, average
quotations falling by almost four per cent from the January
average.

Fertilizer.
It wes reported thut a bill would be presented to the
Diet to authorize the establishment of a company to be
known as the Japan Fertilizer Company and to be capitalized
at fifty million yen, for the purpose of adjusting the
supply of fertilizers, and, in effect, giving it a mono-
polistic control over the business in fertilizer in the
country. The demand for this measure sprang from the
deficiency encountered in the fertilizer supplies of the
country. It was announced in this regard that procuction
of chemical fertilizers has been reduced by some twenty
per cent, there being no prospect of an increase of the
amount in the near future without drastic measures being
undertaken to stimulate the production and to increase the
importation of the necessary raw materials.
Regulations were issued on February 9, for enforcement
on February 26, for the control of tle distribution of
fibre goods. Prices were set on about a thousend articles,
comprising many of daily necessity and use, with the prices
raised generally from around ten to twenty per cent higher
than those of September 18, 1939. Raw silk prices de-
oreased to about yen l625 per bale, or about twenty-rive yen
from those prevailing at the end of January. It should be
noted that in January a drastic reduction in raw silk
prices had occurred from the high peak of almost yen 2400
reached in December.

Prices generally continued their upward march during
February but not in such eccentuated degree as in previous
months. Official figures for wholesele prices were up
only a fraction of a point, while retail prices increased
by more than two per cent. "holesale prices were officially
estimated to have risen by 35.3 per cent from June 1937,
and retail prices by 49.7 per cent. The general cost of
living in Japan, if ceiculated on the basis of official
Not for
prices, should have risen by less than 35 per cent from
the beginning of the "China incident,' but the widespread
attribution
resort, particularly prevalent in the past year, to under-
cover transactions at illegally high prices, accompanied by
all kinds of devices such as short sales, wash seles, de-
posit forfeiture, etc., to evde government economic control
measures and prices, has increased the actual price levels
and the cost of living in Japan to a far greater extent than
the Government figures would show. Noreover, the shortage
of some materials and the inferiority of others would further
tend to throw official statistics out of accurate relation
to the actual situation.*

IX.
LABOR
An ordinance to become effective March 1 was promulgated
under the authority of the Netional General Mobilization Law
whereby male workers between the ages of 1l and 30 and female
workers between the ages of 1l and 20, subject to regulations
covering their employment, could be diverted to the more
Coumince
urgent industries. It was announced that, through this
regulation, industries engaged in the production of non-
essential commodities woulc be forced to decrease their
7
staffs to 70 per cent of the total employed as of December
31,1939.
It was announced in the Diet that a plan for mobilization
of labor was being drafted and would probably be put into
effect in the near future. It was also announced that the
Government expected an early dissolution of labor unions in
the country, as they were felt to be contrary to the spirit
of

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
-42-
of industrial service to the state during an emergency
period. There was s ome discussion in the Diet in regard
to the elleged decline in labor efficiency in Japan recently.
It was considered that this decrease in efficiency was
materially affecting the industrial production plans of the
country. The low standard of wages as compared with the
rising cost of living, the limitations of employment and the
length of the working hours were held to be the principal
causes for the decreased efficiency of labor.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Japanese bombing of Haiphong-Yunnan
Rallway: Foreign Office reply to
Embassy's representations.
On March 6, the Embassy receivea a reply to its
911.94
representations, which, it will be recelied, were made
in January, concerning the Japanese bombing cf the
Haiphong-Yunnan railway. This reply was to the effect
that, through an agreement entered into botween China
and France in 1903, this railway was excluded from any
neutral status in the event of war between China and a
third power and that, as the railway is bein sed as a
supply route for arms, ammuniticn and other military com-
modities, the Japanese forces may properly undertake opera-
tions against it; that no responsibility lies with the
Japanese Government through the prosecution of these
operations against the railway in respect to tre endangering
of American lives and injuring of Americen commerce using
the railway; and that the assurances of the Japanese
Government for the respect of the interests of third powers
in China may not be interpreted as limiting in any way the
proper military actions by the Japanese forces.*
In conformity with the Department's instructions**the
Embassy addressed to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs an
informal communication in rejoinder to the Foreign Office
reply
Embassy's telegram no. 155, March 6, 7 p.m.
Department's telegram no. 85, March 9, 2 p.m.

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
reply, in which it was stated that the American Government
could not admit the relevancy to the question of the ref-
erence to the Chinese-French agreement of 1903 nor could
it admit lack of responsibility on the part of the Japanese
Government for any loss of American life or damage to
American property caused by the current Japanese military
operations in China. Full reservation was then made of
American rights in the matter.*
(b) Reopening of the Yangtze River.
It will be recalled (Reference Monthly Report for
December 1939) that in his conversation with the Ambassador
on December 18, 1939, the then Foreign Minister, Admiral
Nomura, informed Mr. Grew that the Japanese Government
intended to open the Yangtze River for general navigation
as far as Nanking "in about two months." The Ambassador
received word from the Foreign Minister during the early
part of the month that the continued delay in putting these
intentions into effect was due, in large part, to certain
unforeseen c auses, but that preparations for the reopening
of the river were being carried out as rapidly as possible.**
An excuse put forward by the spokesman of the Navy Ministry
for the delay was that the Chinese were floating mines down
the river, making it unsafe for "general" shipping.
On Maroh 4, the Minister of War is reported to have
stated in reply to an inquiry from a Diet member as to the
reopening of the Yangtze River that he assumed that the
river
*mabassy's telegram no. l65, March 12, noon, and
mbassy's despatch no. 4570, March l1, 1940.
mbassy's telegram no. 157, Maron 7, 6 p.m.
e

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
river would be opened "sooner or later," but that this might
not occur at all in the event, for instance, that third na-
tions should obstruct J&panese military operations and main-
tenance of peace and order in China. The spokesman of the
Foreign Office later interpreted the statement of the Yar
Linister to mean that the privilege of navigating the Yangtze
River might, if deemed necessary, not be extended in indi-
vidual cases to shipping of powers acting against Japan, but
that preparations were being continued toward opening the
river. There were no further developments in this matter
during March.
(c) Establishment of the Wang Ching-wei
"regime" at Nanking.
The Secretary of State issued on March 30 a statement
in which was set forth the position of the United States
Government in the light of the establishment on that day at
Nanking of the new régime at Nanking headed by Wang Ching-
wei. After noting that the setting up of the Nanking régime
appeared to be "a further step in a program of one country
by armed force to impose its will upon a neighboring country
and to block off a large area of the world from normal po-
litical and economic relations with the rest of the world,"
and after reiterating the concern of the United States in
the present developments in China, the statement announced
that the United States, believing that the Chinese National
Government still has the allegiance and support of the great
majority of the Chinese people, "of course continues to
recognize that Government as the Government of China."*
(a)
"Depertment's telegram no. 103, March 30, 2 p.m.

(d) Representations made by the Embassy in March
The Embassy made representations to the Foreign Office
in March in the following instances:
1. In regard to restrictions imposed by the Japanese
naval authorities on the commercial activities of merican
oil companies in the Namhoi district near Canton and the
granting of oil monopolies to Japanese-sponsored firms at
the town of Fatshan in that district.*
2. In regard to the damage suffered by the 'merican
Church Mission at Kingtehchen, Kiangsi, as a result of
Japanese bombing of that city.**
It should be noted that during the first quarter of this
year there was, with but one or two exceptions, an absence
of reported damage to American properties as a result of
Japanese military operations in China.
(e) General relations.
The general attitude in Japan towards the United States
during the month under review was much as that manifested in
the previous two months: one of resentment and apprehension.
(Reference monthly reports for January and February 1940.)
The Japanese position is that the key to the "course of things"
lies in doing away with the status quo in the Far East and in
establishing in its stead that vague conception which is termed
the "new order in East Asia." The United States, however, is
unappreciatively and narrow-mindedly endeavoring to stand in
the way of the changes in the status guo; it is opposing Japan
in its "immutable policy" in this regard, and it is
even attacking the Japanese 'hew order," whioh is
already well on the road towards execution. The
press
Dapartment's telegram no. 87, Maroh 11, 6 p.m.;
Mabessy's telegram no. 185, Maroh 20, 11 a.m., and
mbassy's despatch no. 4628, April 4, 1940.
mmgha1's telegram no. 224, March 18, 4 p.m.;
msay's despatoh no. 4613, Maroh 29, 1940.

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
press listed the positive steps that the United States had
taken against Japan as being, in part: the two basic
notes presented to the Jepanese Government in the autumn
of 1938, the State Department's placing of moral embargoes
against the acceptance of orders from Japanese munitions
manufacturers, the extension of the silver purchase agree-
ment, the advancing of trade credits to the Chinese
National Government, the abrogation of the Treaty of Com-
merce and Navigation, and the building programs of the
American navy, strengthening of the naval defenses in the
Pacific Ocean, and the conducting of"intimidating" naval
maneuvers there. It was further added that the United
States had taken over the functions formerly exercised by
Britain and France, before the European war put an end to
their activities, of giving meterial and financial aid to
China. Finally, the United States was seid to be holding
as a trump card the imposition of embargoes against Japan,
regardless of the hardships they would cause to this country.
For all this merican opposition to and menacing of Japan,
it was announced, there was no justifiable cause, as Japan
has been giving every consideration to American rights and
interests in China and is trying to settle the problems
that have arisen there.
The tone of this feeling against the United States be-
came somewhat more intensified, and the press comments con-
cerning Japanese-American relations waxed noticeably harsh
Auring Maroh. It was declared that Japan might well consider
a re-oxaminmtion of its policy towards and dealings with the
tatsed States. The Government was said by the press to be
studying

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
studying all practical counter-mcasures to meet any situation
that might arise. Mention was particularly made cf the fact
that Japan was now looking to its navy as its host Toans and
weapon to deal with the United Stetes.*
On March llth the spokesman of the Foreign ico
stated to foreign press correspond ents that Japan han done
about all there was to do to ease the si turtion between the
two countries and that Japan felt it was high time for the
United States to reciprocate Japan's efforts toward tris end.
He added, in reply to an inquiry as to how the United States
should reciprocate, that Japan did not necessarily expect
the United States "to take an initiative" but rather to show
some definite gesture indicating American desire to improve
relations with Japan. There was no question of Jepen's
being "satisfied" with things as they are at the prosent. he
said in regard to the question of the non-treaty situation
between Japan and America, for despite the fact ther trade
relations had not been visibly affected by the lapso of the
treaty, the uncertainties attending the absence of a defi-
nite agreement were not assuring. He also expressed the
opinion that there are two aspects to American-Japanese
relations. The one concerned American rights and interests
in China; the other concerned principles. If the only issue
between the two countries were the former, it could, he felt,
be

The Foreign Minister also endeavored to allay the
appreciable speculation and agitation that had been mani-
fested in the press and Diet over the purpose of the
visit of the Under Secretary of State's visit to Europe.
It will be recalled that Mr. Welles's visit was considered
here as portending an effort on the part of the United
States to bring about peace in Europe and that the
American Government intended thereafter to interfere to
the same end in the Far East to the disadvantage of Japan.
Moreover, it was noted that the United States had not in-
cluded Japan in its consultations with other powers over
the future economic structure of the world. The Foreign
Minister stated that he understood that r. elles's visit
to Europe was merely to observe an  report on the uropean
situation, that he had no authority to make any overtures
for peace, and that, anyway, it was premature to think that
there were any possibilities of the hostilities in Europe
being concluded at the present time.
A distinct feeling, it mey be noted, again was evident
in this country--one that had been prominent in Japan in the
late spring and summer of 1939 but had been greatly dissi-
pated in the autumn of last year--that the Tnited States would
eventually if not shortly become involved in the uropean
hostilities. The sentiment was as well held with this feel-
ing that if the United States should become involved therein
the grip might be relaxed of the invisible hand of merica
on Japan's shoulder that was held there ready to jerk Japan
baok from its headlong march forward in its China policy.

II. RELATIONS ITH OTHER COUNTRIES
(a) Great Britain
1. Tientsin question.
There had been no significant developments during
the first two months of this year in the negotiations for
741.94
a solution of the Tientsin question which have been carried
on intermittently and laboriously since last summer between
the British Embassy and the Foreign Office.* In March,
however, this matter received much attention and by the end
of the month, after frequent discussions, the negotiations
had progressed to a stage where a settlement of the question
appeared to be in sight. As regards the disposition of the
Chinese silver stored in the British Concession at Tientsin,
the proposal which the British submitted in December of last
year was slightly modified in the light of the changes de-
sired by the Chinese Government** but it remained in most
essentials in its original form. Two points concerning this
issue were still to be resolved: the control that the desig-
nated British and Japanese banks were to have over the silver,
and the giving of a definite undertaking by the Japanese that
after the conclusion of the agreement they will make no
further demands for relief purposes on the stored silver.
The draft formulas concerning the other points in the
Tientsin question which had already been generally agreed
upon but which were not to be adopted until the question.
could be settled as a whole, were also taken up in March.
These
mbassy's telegram no. 25, January 17, 6 p.m., and
Embessy's despatch no. 4422, January 16, 1940.
*mchungking's telegram no. 113, March 4, 1 p.m.

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
These formulas related to: currency, the main feature being
the British undertaking to place no obstacle in the way of
the use of the North China "Federal Reserve Bank" currency
in the Biitish Concession; police matters: and the British
desiderata that all blockade measures and restricticns are
to be removed and that the Japanese will do all in their
power to suppress in regions under their control anti-British
activity and agitation.*
2. General relations.
During the month under review there appeared a marked
note of friendliness towards Great Britain in the press and
other discussions of Anglo-Japanese affairs. This attitude
also was noted in the official approaches of the Japanese
Government to the problems between the two countries. In
various statements made by important personages during the
month, the feeling was expressed that the "worst" in the
relations between the two countries had been passed and the
hope ventured that the two countries would shortly be able
to resolve their differences. Particular emphasis was
placed by the Japanese upon the importance of the economic
relation between the Japanese and Sritish empires, obviously
reflecting the current Japanese concern as regards its
foreign trade; of which trade the British Empire accounts
for a large share.
on
*Imbassy's despatch no. 4546, February 29, 1940;
Embassy's telegrams no. 153, March 5, 4 p.m.;
no. 161, March 9, 3 p.m.; no. 178, March 15, 7 p.m.;
no. 196, March 28, 2 p.m.;
Embassy's despatches
no. 4601, March 19, and no. 4617, March 29, 1940.

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)

On March 28th the 3ritish Ambassador delivered an ad-
dress before the Japan-British Society, in which, while
mentioning in a general way the difficulties that had beset
British-Japanese relations during the past few years, he
spoke in friendly and hopeful terms of the future. He said
in effect that Japan and Britain being two maritime powers
on the fringe of continents were vitally concerned with
those continents and were striving for much the same ob-
jectives, and that it should be possible to bring into
full harmony their national policies.*
The British Ambassador, in a note to Mr. Grew, explained
with regard to the unfavorable comments that had appeared in
the United States press regarding his speech; that his re-
marks portended no change in British policy toward Chine, and
in particular no intention to recognize the new régime that
was being set up at Nanking.**
(b) China
1. Military operations***
The month of March was a very quiet one as far as
793.94
Japanese military operations in China were concerned, and
most of what military activity that occurred was devoted to
clean-up campaigns. These appeared to have been conducted
in numerous areas in much of the occupied territory; many
being repetitions of similar activities conducted in the same
localities during the past two years, where, despite Japanese
efforts, Chinese attacks continued to occur.
In
*mmbassy's despatch no. 4624, April 2, 1940.
**mmbassy's telegram no. 219, April 1, 1 p.m.
Military Attaché.

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
In the northwest, Japanese forces again undertook
operations against Wuyuan, in Suiyuan Province, which the
Chinese had succeeded in recapturing from the Mongolian
garrison left there after the Japanese captured it in
January. The Japanese claimed to have retaken it, but
they again withdrew to Paotow as they had done after their
previous advance. In central China there was little ac-
tivity reported. In south China further fighting occurred
around Nanning; while the Japanese again occupied Chungshan
and also iaunched an offensive neer the border of Macao
in the Pearl River delta.
Japanese reports continued to feature internecine
warfare in China between the Communists and Chinese
National Government forces, particularly in Shansi and
Shantung. The Japanese press reported that this fighting
was being eagerly watched not only by Japan but by Russia
as well. The latter country, according to these reports,
had stationed some six thousand troops at Lanchow, Kansu,
and Hsian, Shensi.
There was very little aerial activity during the month.
2. Establishment of the Wang Ching-wei régime
at Nanking.
On March 12 the Premier issued a long statement which
constituted in fact the prelude for the long-heralded
establishment on March 30th of the Japanese-created new
"Central Chinese Régime". In this statement Admiral Yonai
reiterated the oft-repeated Japanese declaration that the
determined

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
determined policy of Japan was the establishment of
"permanent peace in the Orient" and a "new order in East
Asia." He stated that "in the performance of the sacred
task of reconstructing East sia, the first step to be
taken is to create and insure a new international relation-
ship between Japan, Manchukuo and China." He then
referred to the principles of the Konoe statement on which
this accomplishment was to be based. He announced that
Japan would assist in the formation of a new régime in China
and after its establishment would speedily extend recogni-
tion to it.*
On March 20 the Central Politicel Conference met at
Nanking and approved in short order the prearranged or-
ganization of the new government. The organization of
this "régime" headed by "ang Ching-wei followed closely
that of the Chinese National Government.
With the establishment of this régime the "Renovation
Government" at Nanking and the Provisional Government of
North China were disbanded. The latter, however, was trans-
formed into a North China Political Affairs Commission
located at Peiping, and having jurisdiction over the
three northern provinces of Shansi, Hopeh, and Shantung.
This Commission is to have wide powers and to be inde-
pendent to a large extent of the vang Ching-wei régime,
to allow for a very considerable degree of autonomy in
North
*Embassy's telegram no. 179, March 15, 8 p.m.

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
North China. It is to handle the administration and
economic affairs of that region, maintain its own so-
called "peace preservation army," direct and supervise
the governments of the three provinces, and "deal with
foreign affairs of a local character." The currency of
the Federal Reserve Bank is to be maintained in that area.
The inaugural ceremonies were held as scheduled on
March 30, with Wang Ching-wei announcing the return of
the "National Government" to Nanking, and that it was the
only legal government of China. In a proclamation which
he read on the occasion, he declared that the two points
of the policy of the new government were the realization
of peace through political and economic cooperation with
Japan and the establishment of a constitutional govern-
ment. For the execution of this policy a ten-point
platform was set forth in the proclamation, as follows:
respect for the legitimate rights and interests of
friendly powers; checking of Communism; furthering of
democratic principles; invitation of foreign capitel and
technical assistance for the economic development of the
country; promotion of foreign trade; establishment of
a central bank and unification of the currency system;
and readjustment of taxation. It was also declared that
all regulations and treaties issued in the future by
the Chungking Government would be considered void. The
Chungking Government was invited to participate in the
"peace movement."  decree was later issued calling

Before the Wang Ching-wei 'government"had been in-

(g) India
Several days before the expiration of the Japanese-
Indian trade agreement on March 31, and following five
months of negotiation, a providional trade agreement was
reached with regard to the exchange of Japanese cotton
textiles for Indian raw cotton. No formal trade agreement
was concluded, and negotiations in this latter regard were
postponed. It was reported that the provisional agreement
provided for an increase from the present 283,000,000 yards
to 300,000,000 yards of Japanese textiles, to be exchanged
for one million bales of Indian raw cotton. Moreover,
exports of Japanese textiles could be increased on a sliding
scale up to 400,000,000 yards if 1,500,000 bales of raw
cotton from India were imported. Japanese cotton textiles
exporters were reported to have been not entirely pleased
with the accord, despite the increased quote, as a clause
bad

had been made to counteract the unfavorable position in

themselves due to the fall that had already occurred in the
exchange rate of the rupee.
(h) Netherlands East Indies
Publicity was given during the month under review to
reports that the Japanese Government was preparing to under-
756.94
take shortly negotiations at Batavia with the Netherlands
East Indies for an adjustment of the trade relations between
Japan and the Netherlands possessions. It was stated in
these reports that Japan was motivated in its desire to con-
clude new trede arrangements with the Netherlands Tast Indies
by its needs for raw materials which are obtainable there.
Of secondary importance, it was said, was the hope to be
able to export more goods to the Indies. Japan was particu-
larly looking to the Dutch colony as a large source of oil
and scrap iron and, again, as in its concern over its trade
with other parts of the world, Japan's special attention
was presently being given to these materials, among others,
because of its apprehension that their importation might
possibly be curtailed or shut off from American souroes.
It was also alleged in Japan, that following the out-
break of the European hostilities in the fall of lest year,
the Netherlands authorities had placed the exportation of
o1l, gutta percha, rubber, scrap iron, tin, and certain
other produets under a license system, and had thereafter
bogun

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.98)
-22-
begun to adopt a policy of giving preferential treatment
to the needs of European countries for these products.
This, it is held, has rendered it difficult for Japan to
import the raw materials in such quantities as she re-
quires. It is hoped to alleviate this situation through
the coming negotiations. Some obstacles it is admitted
will be encountered in effecting satisfactory arrangements,
not only because of the colonial economy of the Netherlands
islands and their connection with European countries, but
also because of the strong capitalistic influence of Britain
and the United States there, and, with particular regerd
to petroleum, because the oil fields there, the present and
potential value of which is considered to be very important,
are largely operated by and under the control of British
and American interests. Mention was also made of the in-
sistence in the past on the part of the business interests
in the islands that Japan import Indiea sugar, and it was
predicted that Japan might agree to purchase a certain
amount of sugar if the Netherlands authorities will permit
large exports of the other goods urgently wanted by Japan.
Japanese trade with the Netherlands East Indies has
played an important role in Japan's international commercial
relations. Since the World War that colony has ranked in
never less than fifth place in Japan's export trade, at
times taking more even than China, and last year more than
the whole of Latin America. For years the trade has been
very favorable to this country, last year exports being
elmost double imports in value. The figures for Japanese

(1) Argentina
On March 15, it was announced, a reciprocal trade
agreement was concluded at Buenos Aires between Argentina
and Japan. The agreement provides for a yearly exchange
of Japanese goods to the value of Yen 30,000,000 for
Argentine products of equal value. Japan is to include in
the value freight and insurance charges to Argentina and
exclude those charges in the value of the imports from
Argentina. The barter is to involve some twenty-two articles.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL CONDITIONS
(a) Diet
The high lights in the somewhat uneventful proceedings
of the Diet during March were the ccutinued repercussions
of the Saito incident and the charge made on the floor of
the Lower House that the British expression of regret over
the ASAMA MARU incident was Eained by a promise of the
Government not to transport German passengers of military
age on Japanese ships.
After further discussion during the first few days of
the month and certain changes in the composition of the
disciplinary committee of the Diet, that committee unani-
mously voted on March 6 for Mr. Saito's expulsion, and on
the next day its recommendation was approved by the Diet
in oamera.*
As a manifestation to the country and to the world of
Japanese solidarity and unity of purpose, a resolution was
unanimously passed on March 9 by the Diet pledging united
support to the Government's "definite and unalterable"
polioy regarding the "China incident."
The
*mbassy's despatch no. 4602, March 20, 1940.

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
The Prime Minister expressed at the closing meeting
of the Diet the Governmont's complete satisfaction with the
accomplishments of its 75th session, but the vernacular
press generally held that the session was a comparatively
barren one. Despite the fact that over a hundred legisla-
tive measures were enacted, the proceedings were claimed
to have accomplished little of a constructive nature. The
Diet members were chastized for meaningless interpellations
that were often directed more at heckling the Government
than eliciting informative replies on importent matters.
Complaint was also made of the deep secrecy with which the
Government insisted on shrouding the vital affairs of the
country from knowledge of the Diet and the people.
(b) Political parties
Reverberations from the Saito case were evident in all
the political parties, and the cleavage in political thought
brought into the open by Mr. Caito's remarks cut indiscrimi-
nately across party lines. One or two members resigned from
the Minseito, another group tendered their resignation from
the Kuhara faction of the Seiyukai, but through the efforts
of the party president, their differences with the party were
finally made up. The Social Mass Party, however,
split
up, with its president, r. Isao Abe,leading nine other mem-
bers out of the party, reducing its size to negligible
proportions. Indications in the background were also seen
of a revival of the movement for the formation of a new
national party.*
Further
mbessy's despatoh no. 4602, March 20, 1940

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
-26-
Further disturbing effects were left by remarks
made by Major-General Akira Muto of the ar Office, when
on Marcih 19 he stated that the Army was determined to
seak, "y legal means", the dissolution of political
parties which fail to rid themselves of self-interest
and to cooperate in carrying out the national policies.
General Muto, in his address before a committee of the
Diet, acting apparently as spokesman for the Army, stated
that "all kinds of liberalism based on individualism"
must be set aside during the present emergency, in order
that Japan may achieve a concerted and united national
purpose. He said that in the world of today nationalistic
ideas and conceptions must prevail in all that pertains to
national security and economy, and that, finally, for
Japan to pass through the present situetion successfully,
it must adhere to the idea generally known abroad as
totalitarianism.
The primary cause for the Army's present disfavor of
the political parties, which General Muto's remarks were
interpreted as implying, lay evidently in Mr. Saito's
inquiry into the Government's policy for the prosecution
of the China incident and the consequent difference of
opinion.
(c) Position of the Government
The Government was considered to have come through
the Diet period in fairly good order. Some criticism was
voioed that it had managed to hold on by "muddling through,"
using the Saito affair as a fortuitous means to get needed
support. It was predicted that the coming few months
would

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
-27-
would be hazardous ones for the Government in its imple-
mentation of its policies concerning internal economic
problems and foreign affairs.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION
The Diet enacted 134 of the 136 bills presented by the
Government. Twenty-six measures concerned the budget and
other fiscal matters and were passed without amendment.
Forty-five tax bills were also approved but with some amend-
ments. There were a number of revisions of existing laws.
The principal important new measures enacted were: ooal
distribution control law, charcoal supply and distribution
law, law for the establishment of a Government fertilizer
company, law for the inspection of agricultural products,
law for the promotion of the export of agricultural products,
law for the extension of export subsidies to manufacturers;
law for state control of casualty reinsurance, and a law for
the sterilization of the hereditary insane and feeble-minded.
There was a good deal of opposition over the coal distribu-
tion law providing for Government subsidies to coal companies.
It was criticized as being a futile gesture that would entail
a waste of money.

was reported at 81%. In the 1939-1940 fiscal year ending
Maroh 31, the amount of domestic bonds outstanding increased
by Yen 5,562,935,000, bringing the domestic national indebted-
ness to Yen 21,628,073,000. Japan's foreign indebtedness

Budget and tax program for fiscal year l940-41.
The final budget estimates as approved by the Diet
provided for Government expenditures in the next fiscal
year of Yen 10,557,000,000. Through the addition of two
supplementary amounts, the original estimates proposed
by the previous cabinet were increased by Yen 275,000,000.
The coming year's estimates exceeded by over a billion,

4,460,000,000 was contained in the speciel account for
the financing of the China incident, the remainder in
the general accounts. Some seventy per cent of the
entire budget was reportedly to be for military
expenditures.
Slightly over Yen 6,000,000,000 of the total amount
is to be raised by new bond issues during the coming
fiscal year. To absorb surplus currency and attract
funds from small investors a bill was enacted to provide
for the issuance of some Yen 500,000,000 in semi-1ottery
bonds which by draw would pay large premiums.
Question arose in the Diet whether the amount of the
budget would cover the government expencitures planmed
for the new fiscal year, as the budget had been compiled
on the basis of the average commodity prices for the
latter part of l938, whereas since that time prices have
comsiderably risen, reportedly by more than thirty per cent.
pears

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
-29-
Fears were also expressed that the continued dis-
bursement of large government funds is liable to push
the country dangerously close to the brink of "vicious"
inflation.
Further note was made that the increase in the past
few years for military expenditures would be permanent
and were expected by the Government itself to continue
to rise further in the future. The Prime Minister
stated, concerning this observation, that the Government
felt that the economic strength of the country was such
that it could bear the present burden and further demands
on it besides as time goes on.
To realize the more than four billion yen revenue
that estimatedly would be obtained from taxes, sweeping
increases in taxation, both horizontal to cover more
sources of revenue and vertical to enlarge the revenue
from direct and indirect taxes already imposed, was
resorted to. A far-reaching system of graduated and
surtax income taxes was set up; while many forms of
corporate and corporate profit, luxury, transaction,
and sales taxes were either increased or newly imposed.
It was estimated that the yield on the income tax would
be equivalent to Yen 54 per capita. In the final com-
promise made by the Government to meet the objections of
the Diet, among other changes, the basic tax exemption
limit was raised on wages from Yen 600 to Yen 720 annually
and on income from business from Yen 400 to Yen 500.
In order to meet the increased demand for funds
next year, the goal for national saving was set at
twelve billion yen, two billions above that set for the
yoar

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)

year just ended. A fixed limit of one billion yen
for Japan proper and eight hundred million yen for
"Manchukuo" was to be made for funds to be raised by
private business through debentures. This repre-
sented a curtailment from that of the past year of
an estimated thirty per cent.
On March 2, the Finance Minister stated to the
Diet that the national indebtedness that was being
created "no matter how much it increases," will be
easily liquidated with the expansion of the nation's
economic resources resulting from the economic associa-
tion of Japan, "Manchukuo", and China.
VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC ANL FININCLAL CONDITIONS*
It was generally felt that the scope and intensity
of official control would be greatly extended from the
beginning of the 1940-4l fiscal year beginning April 1.
The Commerce and Industry Minister stated that the
Government intended soon to have a series of measures
ready for the adjustment of virtually all essential
prices, production expension, restricted consumption,
and improved distribution.
There were a number of new regulations imposed
during March. Upward price revisions were made covering
a wide range of commodities; control over the distribution
of sulphate of ammonia and other chemical fertilizers
and industrial chemicals was established; control over
iron and steel was also strengthened, with a new official
organization

=== Page 189 === (confidence 1.00)

organization to be known as the Japan Iron and Steel
Association formed to regulate production and distri-

Business was reportedly active during the month,
and with an improvement in the electric power and coal
situation industrial activity increased over that for
the previous month. The stock market was firm.
The weakness of sterling and the rise of the yen-
sterling rate to ls.4d. on March l8th caused considerable
uneasiness in Japanese export circles in view of the
importance of the British Empire market in Japan's foreign
trade. Toward the close of the month there was con-

diate steps to cope with the unfavorable situation to
Japan's exports threatened by the weakness of the pound.
Retail commodity prices and living costs showed
further advances during March, in continuetion of the
trend which has been evident, despite the Government's
efforts to check it, since the beginning of the China
hostilities. Retail prices according to Government
statistics were up by l.8 per cent over those for the
previous month, the cost of living by a little over
1.5 per cent. This brought the increase since June 1937
as officially estimated by the Government, to 5l.5% for

to have deolined by .5 per cent during the month, redueing
the officially estimated rise in that index since June
1937 to 34.6 per cent.

Foreign trade
Preliminary foreign trede figures for arch
placed the value of exports at Yen 367,869,C00 and
imports at Yen 379,60l,000 which represented a sub-
stantial increase in both categories over the previous
month and 30 per cent increase in exports and a 35
per cent gain in imports over the corresponding month
of the previous year. Japan proper's trade with the
yen bloc, including Hong Kong, amounted in exports to
Yen 181,393,000 and in imports to Yen 77,094,000,
representing an increase of 50 per cent in exports and
30 per cent increase in imports
the year before.
Japan's foreign trade for the first quarter of
the year amounted to Yen 918,000,000 for exports and
Yen 991,058,000 for imports, leaving an unfavorable
balance of Yen 72,000,000. The total trade for this
period showed an increase of slightly over 30 per cent
as compared with the corresponding three months of
last year. On the face of these figures it would appear
that a considerable improvement had been made in Japan's
foreign trade but the increase was accounted for prin-
cipally in value, through the 15% depreciation of the
yen and higher prices for many commodities. The actual
trade by volume was little changed. As regards Japan's
trade with foreign currency countries (those outside of
the ven bloc), Japan had for the three months an un-
favorable balance of Yen 282,033,000 as compared with an
unfavorable balance of Yen 199,091,000 for the corres-
ponding period of last year.

Japan proper's trade with the yen bloc, including

imports. Imports increased only by some forty million yen
over the corresponding period of the year before but
exports were vastly increased by over one hundred fifty
million yen.

I. RELATIONS ITH THE UNITED STATES
(a) Japanese reaction to the Secratary of State'
statement on March 30, concerning the
Nanking régime
On April 1, the spokesman of the Foreign Office
commented in a press interview upon Mr. Hull's statement
of March 30 concerning the establishment of the "new
central régime" at Nanking. After saying that corsider-
ing the United States Government's attitude toward the
China Affair the statement was a natural outcome, he
proceeded to assail it as being 'blind and inconsistent"--
blind to "the new developments taking place in China" and
inconsistent with the American Government's note of De-
cember 30, 1938, to the Japanese Government in which it was
declared that the United States "is well aware of the changes
that are taking place in the Far East." Mr. Suma further
declared that the statement disregards the efforts that
Japan has been making and the assurances it has Eiven to
ameliorate Japanese-American relations instead of being
patient and waiting a reasonable length of time for the
assurances to materialize; that it is not true, as the
statement charges, that the Japanese Army placed ang
Ching-wei in his present position or that Japan is attempting
to set up an economic bloc against the rest of the world;
and that, as the statement will instigate anti-Japanese
groups in the United States and will also be a factor in
immensely encouraging the Chungking Government, against
which Japan is engaged in hostilities, it is incompatible
with the holding of Japanese-American discussions in a
constructive spirit.*
The
*Tmbassy's telegram no. 222, April 1, 7 p.m.

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The Foreign Minister merely stated that the Secretary's
statement was not unexpected, that if the new government
enjoys the confidence of the Chinese people, in his view
third powers cannot but recognize it in the end, and that
at any rate Japan must exert its efforts towards bringing
about a status of the new régime which merits recognition.*
The press also showed little surprise over the Secretary's
statement, asserting that it was to be expected, and in its
comments generally reiterated the sentiments expressed by
the Foreign Minister.
.Some mention wes made of the United
States being quick to set forth its position as regards the
Wang régime--the United States had taken an obdurate,
doctrinarian and uncompromising position in the matter in
contrast with Great Britain and France, which countries,
although they mey not be expected to recognize the new
régime, can probably be counted on to a certain extent to
cooperate with the Nanking régime primarily for the purpose
of protecting their interests in China.**
(b) American and Japanese relations with the
Netherlands East Indies
There was published in full in Japan the statement of
the Secretary of State released on April 17 referring to
the statement made by the Japanese Minister for Foreign
Affairs on April 15, in which the latter expressed concern
on the part of the Japanese Government for the maintenence
of the status guo of the Netherlands East Indies.
There
*Embassy's telegram no. 218, April 1, noon.
**mbassy's telegrams no. 226, April 2, 5 p.m., and
no. 228, Apr11 3, 10 a.m.

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
There was no official comment on Mr. Hull's statement,
the press announcing in this regard that the Japanese
Goverment had decided that such was not called for. The
vernacular newspapers, however, were voluble on the subject.
One paper observed that, although Mr. Hull's statement
was substantially similar to that of Mr. Arita, the American
attitude implied in the statement--of interfering unneoes-
sarily in the estern Pacific--is "unfriendly" and "unin-
telligible." The Tashington Conference treaties could not
be considered to be any longer applicable. The United
States should realize that Japan has much more intimate and
important connections with the Netherlands Indies than has
the United States. The Netherlands Government's expression
of appreciation of and complete agreement with the Japanese
Covernment's attitude was, moreover, to be considered as
the only important factor and rendered M1. Hull's "third
party statement unnecessary.*
(See section herein below concerning Japanese relations
with the Netherlands East Indies)
(c) Japanese attitude concerning Philippine
immigration legislation
Concern and agitation were manifested in Japan during
the month under review regarding the immigration bill being
deliberated by the Philippine National Assembly providing
for a restriction of the total number of immigrants of each
nationality to five hundred each year. On that day it was
reported in the press that Japan had lodged a protest with
the
*mbassy's telegrams no. 265, April 19, 12 p.m.;
no. 273, April 22, 1 p.m.; no. 276, Apr1l 22, 10 p.m.;
Imbassy's despatch no. 4667, April 23, 1940.

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
the United States Government at ashington against the
restriction of Philippine immigration to five hundred
nationals of each country "without providing for special
consideration for Japarese." This "special consideration"
it was stated arose from an understanding said to have been
reached last year between Mr. Suma, then Counselor of
Embassy at ashington, and an official of the Division of
Far Eastern Affairs of the State Department. In the same
press report Mr. Suma, the spokesman of the Foreign Office,
was quoted to the effect that the number of Japanese immi-
grants to the Philippines during the last few years had
averaged almost twenty-eight hundred; that, moreover,
while Japan welcomed the absence of racial or national
discrimination in the bill, the present measure would in
effect injure the interests of Japan; and that Japan is
justified in expecting the Philippine Legislature to take
into consideration the contribution made by the Japanese to
the prosperity of the islands.
It is to be noted that the Japanese Ambassador at
Washington called at the Lepartment on April 20 and requested
that the Department urge the Philippine Government to allow
nationals of Japan, along with those of all other countries,
to immigrate into the Philippines in the number of one
thousand instead of five hundred and that the request was
transmitted to the Philippine Government. No commitment
was made by the Department to sponsor an exception in favor of
Japan as regards Philippine immigration. According to the
statistics compiled by the Philippine authorities, the average
net immigration from Japan during the last thirty-five year
period was only 640 persons and for the last fifteen years
only 930 persons.*
The
*Department's telegram no. 124, April 22, 7 p.m.

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
The press reported that Japan was watching the situa-
tion and would follow future developments with great care.
In the original press report on the matter and in several
press editorials it was stated that the United States
directly participated in the work of drafting the measure,
which is regarded as a part of "a series of acts of
oppression against Japan", including American naval
maneuvers, assistance to the Chineee Government through
commercial loans, the American attitude of "meddling into
the question of the Netherlands Indies," et cetera.*
(d) Bombings of American properties in China.
In conformity with the Department's instructions**
the Ambassador, on April 23, orelly to the Vice Minister
for Foreign Affairs, and in a first person note to the
Foreign Minister emphatically protested against the recent
recrudescence of Japanese bombings of American properties
in China. In these representations there were emphasized
the gravity of the incidents and the concern of the United
States over continuance of the attacks of the Japanese mili-
tary on American properties.***
No publicity was given in Japan either to the bombing
incidents or to the Ambassador's representations.
(e) Continued Japanese interference at Tientsin
with American rights and interests.
On April 23 the Ambassador, conformably with the
Department's instructions**** made emphatic representations

(g) General relations
At the end of March and in the first part of April much
attention was given to the annual American naval maneuvers,
conducted this year in the Pacific Ocean. Reports appeared
in the press that the maneuvers might be extended beyond
the international date line into the western Pacific area.
Much note was also taken of the continued discussions in
the United States over the question whether or not the
naval

navy had no comment to make, but that if they extended west
of that line they would be regarded as aggressive. The tone
of the press in comment ing on this sub ject was resentful and
defiant. The view was expressed that the maneuvers were

All Japanese newspapers published in front page

was some comment to the effect that they constituted a
further aggravation of American-Japanese relations, but
little discussion was given to the matter.**

toward the United States was further aggravated during the

Pacific Ocean, the United States policy of non-recognition
of the "Nanking régime," the Philippine immigration ques-

America is playing the role of "the watchdog of the

that, if it was too much to expect that all the issues
between the two countries could be forthwith resolved, at
least the relations might be kept from being further impaired.
II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES
(a) Great Britain
1. Tientsin question.
An agreement was reached during the month under review
741.94
1
on the draft formula for the disposition of the Chinese
silver in the British Concession at Tientsin. Further
points still remained to be settled as regards the police
question. The agreement with regard to the silver, how-
ever, was not to become effective until all the outstanding
issues, including the British desiderate for lifting the
blockade, anti-British propaganda, etc., are settled. In
regard to the latter issues, it appesred that the Japanese
were showing some reluctance to implement their assurances
concerning the British desiderata, the Japanese authorities
at Tientsin seemingly being unwilling to lift the blockade
completely or immediately when agreement is reached. It
was reported in the press in the latter part of the month
that the Japanese army authorities at Tientsin asserted
that the blockade was instituted as an independent act
as a result of military necesaity and that, therefore, it
devolved

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
devolved upon them to determine whether it should be lifted.
It was understood, nevertheless, that the Japanese Govern-
ment had expressed the desire to have the blockade removed
onceafor all and that preparatory measures to that end were
and
being undertaken.*
The British Ambassador on April 23 made representations
to the Foreign Office in regard to the treatment by the
Japanese military of British nationals at the barriers of
the British Concession at Tientsin. He stated that the
situation there was steadily beaoming worse, and he added
that, in the face of the continual anti-British activities
of the Japanese forces in China, Great Britain was be-
ginning to wonder whether the British conciliatory policy
towards Japan was justified.**
2. Norwegian and Danish shipping.
Following the German invasion of Norway and Denmark,
there was speculation over British attitude concerning
Norwegian and Danish shipping operating in Far Eastern
waters. Japan had on charter some sixteen Norwegian and
Danish ships, and many vessels of those two countries
regularly call at Japanese ports. Concern was expressed
whether the British would attempt to seize the chartered
ships and to divert the rest of the shipping of those two
countries away from Japanese trade. On April 22, an
arrangement was reported to have been reached between the
British and the Japanese whereby Norwegian vessels would be
permitted by the British and Norwegian authorities to pro-
ceed to Japanese ports and to engage in trade between Japan
and
