3. General relations.
Indications continued of relaxation of the somewhat
tense relations between Japan and Great Britain to which
reference was made in the monthly report for March.
Articles in the press reported that England generally was
showing a tendency toward more "cooperation" with Japan
than it has for some time in the past. Suggestions were
made of possibilities of settlin the problems between
the two countries concerning China and the Far East in a
way that could be constructively favorable to Japan's "new
order" policy. It was observed that Britain's conflict
with Germany was a strongly compelling reason for Great
Britain's desire to better its relations with this country.
This same consideration wes also largely attributed as
motivating a proposal, which the press reported Great
Britein had made to Japan, to enter into extensive trade
arrangements with this country. With the exception of
the announcement that British-controlled oil companies in
Iran had agreed to sell a considerable quantity of petro-
leum to Japan, no official statements, however, were made
as to any trade arrangements being contemplated or under
nogotiation.

(b) China
1. Military operations.*
The only notable development in Japanese military
operations during the month was a fairly large-scale
793.94
campaign in southeastern Shansi. The object of the
campaign was again to clean out Chinese troops from the
area and possibly to occupy the southern part of Chansi
alorg the Yellow River, and to hold the line of the
river with adequate garrisons. Little military activity
was reported as heving occurred in other parts of China.
2. General relations.
In accordance with arrangements announced in March,
General Abe, accompanied by a large delegation, left
Japan in the middle of the month and proceeded to Nanking,
for the purpose of tendering, as ambassador of Japan,
felicitations to the Tang Ching-wei régime and negotiating
"a basic adjustment of Sino-Japanese relations."**
There was some comment in the Japanese press on the
failure of the Japanese to elect five members to the
Municipal Council of the International Settlement at
Shanghai in the election that was held there on April 12.
In some quarters the election of only two Japanese was
attributed to clever manipulation on the part of the British
and also to the lack of Japanese experience in voting.
Further difficulties were predicted for the future but the
hope was expressed that the combined efforts of the Japanese
would in the future improve the Japanese position in the
Settlement.***

(c) France
The French Anbassador protested during the month
under review to the Foreign Office several instances of
flights of Japanese planes over French Indo-Chinese
territory.*
(d) Soviet Russia
It was reported in the press on April 17 that the
Japanese .mbassador at Moscow was called to the Soviet
Foreign Office on April 14 and overtures were made to the
Japanese Government through him for consideration of a
"permanent adjustment of Japanese-Soviet relations." The
press stated that Japanese official reaction to the Joviet
approach was one of doubt concerning its sincerity, that
Russia had a way of assuming a conciliatory attitude towards
Japan when it was or was about to be involved in efforts
concerning other parts of the world, as during the Soviet-
Finnish conflict, but that thereafter such an attitude was
often shortly abandoned.**
(e) Netherlands East I..dies

featured articles stressing at much length thet the Japanese

Netherlands East Indies with the uropean hostilities. It
was stated that there appeared to be a likelihood that the
Netherlands would shortly become enmeshed in the war in
Europe and that in such an event Japan could not but view
with serious concern any repercussions therefrom that might
affect the present status of the Dutch possessions in the

(f) Latin America
The Mexican economic mission which, it will be recalled.
had been invited to come to Japan, arrived here on April
1l for e stay of approximately a month. A number of dis-
cussions were held by it with Japanese trade organs to
explore the possibilities of increasing trade between
the two countries. It was understood, however, that no
official trade agreement would be concluded in Japan and
that any trade arrangements .to be entered into between
the two countries would be negotiated at Mexico City.*
During the month a small economic delegation from
Paraguay also arrived in Jepan and held discussions here
on trade matters.
The press announced that, after several months of
negotiation at Bogotá, an understanding has been reached
for the conclusion of a trade agreement between Japan and
Colombia and that under the new arrangement Japanese ex-
ports to Colombia will expand from the present approximately
half a million yen to seven or eight million yen and imports
from less than two hundred thousand yen at present to be-
tween two and three million yen. The Colombian Legation
here, however, has stated to the mbassy that according
to information received from its Government there has been
no arrangement or trade treaty concluded between the two
countries, and that, although the Japanese have submitted
certain trade proposals to the Colombian Government, there
has been no negobiation thus far on them.**

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS
(a) Foreign Office
It was announc ed during the month under review that
the Ministry of Foreign Affairs is to be expanded to an
appreciable extent during the coming fiscal year begin-
ning April 1. The most important project provided for is
the establishment of a South Seas bureau staffed by a
director, two section chiefs, and a number of secretaries.
Some fourteen new consulates and consulates general are
to be established in different parts of the world.
(b) Army
It was announced that a six-year program was insti-
tuted with the beginning of the fiscal year for the pur-
pose of armament replenishment and structural reform of
the army. The press nlso reported that the Army expected
that, for the next few years at least, military expendi-
tures must be at about as large a figure as that for the
present year.
(c) Official price control and regulation
of national economy
In an effort to check the various undesirable economic
trends and in view of the demand for drastic and immediate
action in this regard, the new economic advisory council,
the Price Tolicy Inquiry Commission, was instructed by the
Government at its initial meeting in the first part of the
month to draft adequate measures to regulate the supply
and demand of daily necessities and to formulate en
acceptable price control policy as soon as possible. It
appeared very likely from reports of the proceedings of
the commission and pronouncements by Government officials
that, as one means of controlling distribution of daily
necessities

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
necessities and essential industrial materials, a system
of rationing, which has already been epplied to certain
commodities, such as matches and steel materials, for a
wide range of commodities would be instituted. Moreover,
the penal provisions of the economic control regulations
will be strengthened in an effort to curb the violations
which have tended to undermine the regulations in the
past. No final decision apparently had been reached by
the end of the month upon the policy to be pursued towards
the control of commodity prices.
In reports appearing in the press toward the end of
the month it was announced that the army, having become
impatient with the delay in the formulation of a concrete
price policy by the Government, and having regard to the
confused and unsatisfactory operation of the present
regulations, had decided to step in and take a hand
itself in the matter. The militery authorities announced
in this connection that they intended to regulate the
profits of firms engeged in the production of munitions
and allied equipment from July l. It was generally antici-
pated that this move would develop eventually to the
placing of all industries under profit control, It was
held that by this means t he army hoped to be able to force
down the prices for military goods and in sympathy there-
with the prices of other £oods. The huge profits currently
being made by industry and the consequent disbureement
in the country of the large sums earned by industry have
been a large contributory factor. to higher prices. It
was felt that the army, in order to have its budget cover
its requirements, was forced to put a stop to the rise in
prioes

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-19-
prices, as prices have gone up so much from the price
level on the basis of which the buaget was computed a
year and a half ago, that the army is finding diffi-
culty in keeping within its budget. It was held, how-
ever, in industrial circles that the ermy's action might
well result in major changes in the capitalistic system.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
Nothing to report
V. GOVERNMENT FINANCE
The first of the new bond issues scheduled for the
new fiscal year was offered in April to the amount of
four hundred million yen. The rate of bond absorption
894.51
for April was better than that for March, reportedly being
90.8%, but far below that for the previous year. Appre-
ciable concern was shown over the failure of the country
adequately to absorh the bonds being floated by the Govern-
ment, and despite the prediction made by the Government
in 1939, that Japan would not have to resort to compulsory
savings, a step in that direction appeared to be envisaged
through a semi-compulsory system of savings by means of
deductions from salary at the source. It was reported
that the system is to be instituted in the near future.
Japan's note issue again increased during April,
reaohing an "all time high.'

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
VI. G NERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS*
The spread of the European hostilities to the
Scandinavian countries in April and the increasing likeli-
hood of other countries in Europe becoming involved in
the war created a profound impression in Japan. For, in
taking account of the resulting effects on Japan's foreign
trade it was generally realized that any advantages which
might be anticipated from this development would probably
be more than outweighed by concurrent disadvantages. A
favorable aspect was considered to be the opportunity for
Japanese manufacturers to capture the export markets for-
merly dominated by the belligerent countries now preoccu-
pied with the war. As against this, however, the curtailing
of the European markets, if not the entire
elimination
of trade with a number of the European countries, and the
restrictions on trade imposed in the belligerent countries
and in their empires, would seriously affect the volume of
Japanese foreign trade. Japan has been importing certain
quantities of wood pulp for the manufacture of rayon, ores
and metals, and machinery from the Scandinavian countries.
Over fifty per cent of Japan's imports of wood pulp from
other than yen bloc countries was obtained last year from
Scandinavia. It became apparent as regards Japanese trade
with the two most important belligerent netions and their
empires, Great Britain and France, where goods in large
quantities have been exchanged with Japan, that there was
a strong possibility of intensified Anglo-French exchange
control which would curtail shipments to the sterling bloo
and
*Prepared with the assistance of the Office of the
Commeroial Attaché.

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
-21-
and that the diversion of the production of the empires
to the needs of the mother countries in prosecuting the
war, and the curtailment, and even banning, of the sale
of many materials to outside nations would greatly reduce
their exports to this country. The trade of the British
Empire with Japan has accounted in the past years for some
thirty per cent of Japan's total foreign trade. The fact,
moreover, of the increasing possibility that the war would
become a protracted one seemingly could only portend con-
tinued aggravation of the situation in the future. It
was felt, in particular, that it would become increasingly
more difficult and considerably more expensive for Japan
to purchase the raw materials and plant equipment needed
in prosecuting the warfare in China, its industrial ex-
pansion program, and its plans for military replenishment.
Despite the arrangements made with Great Britain for
Norwegian ships to continue calling at Japanese ports, the
prospect of a drastic curtailment of Scendinavian shipping
in the Pacific Ocean was viewed with some concern in the
light of the acute shortage of Japanese bottoms available
for the transportation of the foreign raw materials which
are required by the nation's heevy industries.
Internal economic developments also appeared to have
become more unfavorable during April. Living costs and
retail commodity prices continued to surge upward dospite
the official efforts to keep prices down. The trend toward
inflation became more pronounced with the increase in the
daily note issue of the Bank of Japan. The prevailing
shorteges of daily necessities, although they had not be-
oome much worse than during the pest few months, were not
alleviated

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
-22-
alleviated to any extent during the month. Stocks of
goods for export continued to accumulate and the situs-
tion in the cotton textile trade was regarded as being
so unsatisfactory that spinners decided on a voluntary
curtailment of production. This latter situetion has
been attributed in some part to the bans and rustrictions
placed by European countries and their colonial possessions
on imports of Japanese cotton piece goods. It was re-
ported during the month that the productive capacity
expansion program of the country was found to be slowing
down from the high rate maintained in the past. Up to
this year, however, much progress in this line undoubtedly
has occurred and the country has already succeeded in
building up its industrial productive capacity to a very
appreciable extent.
Approximately l40 new laws and ordinances relating
to business, finance, and industry were promulgated during
April. Most of the new measures provided for the es-
tablishment of standard prices for various commodities.
It was announced during the month that a merger would
be effected of the Mitsui firms and their various enormous
and widely diversified interests in Japan and abroad. The
object of : this merger, which is to become effective in
August, reportedly is to permit the utilization of the
single company to further the development of Japanese
foreign trade. The Government evidently will have a large
measure of control over and direction of the business
operations of the company and the operations will be in-
tegrated more closely with the Government's economic policies,
Preliminary

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.98)
-23-

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES
(a) Visit to Japan of the United States High
Commissioner to the Philippines, Mr. Franois
B. Sayre.
Prior to the arrival in Japan of Mr. Francis B. Sayre,
United States High Commissioner to the Philippines, Mr.
Arita, Minister for Foreign Affairs, had indicated to the
Ambassador his desire to meet Ir. Sayre during his visit
to Japan. On May lst Mr. Sayre, accompanied by the Ambas-
sador, paid a courtesy call on the Minister for Foreign
Affairs. At the conclusion of their conversation, Mr. Arita
expressed the hope that he might have an opportunity for
a further talk with Mr. Sayre and subsequently asked the
High Commissioner to call on May 2nd. The press reporting
Mr. Sayre's first interview with the Minister suggested
the possibility that conversations between these two in-
dividuals might lead to a fresh approach to the difficulties
facing Japanese-American relations. Newspapers pointed out
that in spite of efforts on both sides rolations between
Japan and the United States had recently worsened. The hope
was expressed that such conversations might at least provide
the basis for future talks between the Minister for Foreign
Affairs and the Ambassador.
During tho first conversation on May lst, remarks were
devoted almost exclusively to the Philippine immigration
b ill. Mr. Sayro explained that the Philippine Assembly

(e) Terrorist Activities at Shanghai.*
As instructed by the Department, the Embassy made in-

which the latter had received relative to the activities
of terrorists at Shanghai and asked whether instructions
had been received from the Foreign Office in London to

British mbassy replied that it had received no recent in-

I. RELATIONS ITH OTFR COUNTRIES
(a) Netherlands Bast Indies.*
During the month of May the Japanese vernacular press
continued to stress that any change in the status guo of
7542.94
the Netherlands Indies or the placing of those areas under
Amerioan or British control would bring serious consequences.
On May 1l the Minister for Foreign Affairs informed the
Ambassador that he had called in the Gernan Ambessador as
well as the British and French Ambassadors and had told
them that recent events in Europe have accentuated the con-
cern of the Japenese Government in maintaining the status
quo of the Netherlands East Indies. The Ambassador was
later informed by the British Embassy that the British Coun-
selor had been told by the Minister for Foreign Affairs that,
if as a result of the Netherlands becoming involved in the
war in Europe, the supplies which Japan was now receiving
from the Netherlands East Indies should be diminished a
situation would arise which Japan "could hardly bear".
On May 13 the vernacular press devoted consideerable
space to the question of the Netherlands Indies and prominently
reported Secretary of State Hull's statement of May ll. Con-
siderable agitation in "official and unofficial circles" was
reported as a result of the Netherlands Government allowing
the

no intention of intervening in the Netherlands East Indies.

of the supplies of raw materials which Japan receives from
those islands.

(b) Great Britain.*
During the month of May, Anglo-Japenese relations
centered about efforts to effect a settlement of the so-cal-
led Tientsin question, and efter considerable jockeying on
both sides praotical. agreement was reached by the end of the
month on the question of the wording of various confiden-
tial oral and written memoranda and engagements which were to
be exchanged at the time of the announcement of the set-
tlenent of the question. This announcement was not forth-
coming during the month of May, but the British authorities
expressed optimism that the Tientsin question would be solved
in the very near future. The delay was probably due in part
to the technical difficulties involved in reaching agreement
on minor points and in part to the overshadowing importance
of events in Europe.

1. Military Operations*
The prinoipal military operations during the month
of May by the Japanese forces centered in the Honan-Hupeh
area. Beginning on May 1 and lasting for approximately ten
days, the Japanese army began from a line along Sinyang-
Suisien-Chungshang an attempted envelopment along the Tang
and Fai Rivers. The Japanese employed eight divisions and
in their reports claimed success. From experience, however,
competent observers are of the opinion thet while the Japa-
nese may have been successful, the degree of such success
is problematical. The Chinese forces usually escape. There
followed a lull of approximately nine days during which time
the Japanese withdrew to the vicinity of Tsaoyang.
The secona stage of movements in this area began about
May 19 and continued until May 23. Four or five divisions
were used by the Japanese. This second operetion was a
repeated envelopment maneuver on a smaller scale carrying
the Japanese foroes still further beyond the Tang and Pai
Rivers. These operations were well supported by the Army
air foroes.

2. Air Activities.
The principal target for the Japenese air forces (com-
prised largely of naval units) was Chungking. The bombings
of Chungking, which had begun in April, were continued
throughout the month of lay whenever weather conditions were
satisfactory for aerial operations.
(d) France*
Under date of May 3, May 6, and May 7, 1940, the French
Ambassador addressed strong notes to the Japanese Govern-
ment protesting against Japanese actions in the bombing of
the Yunnan Railway and violating French Indo-Chinese ter-
ritory. The representations pointed out that conspicuous
frontier markings had been set up to make impossible future
mistakes concerning the location of the border. A statement
of claime for compensation for French and colonial citizens
killed or wounded by the Japanese bomberdment of the Yunnan
Railway on February 1, 1940, was also presented.
Information reached the mbassy during the month of May
to the effeot that the French Government was prepared to
conolude with the Japenese Government an arrangement con-
cerning the French Concession at Tientsin similar to that
oonoluded between the British end Japanese Governments in
respect of the British Concession. The Frenoh agreement
would apply in part to the question of silver in the Frenoh
concession and the raising of the blockade around the foreign
coneessions.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS
(a) Cabinet.
The press announced on May 27 that conferences be-
tween Prime Minister, the Minister for Foreign Affairs,
Warand Navy Ministers, were to be held at regular inter-
894.882
vals in the future. At these conferences, it was reported,
the four Ministers would discuss and decide upon high po-
licies for the settlement of the China incident and with
regard to the oourse Japan is to follow in the rapidly
changing international conditions. Other State Ministers
may also be asked to attend the conferences from time to
time. The newspaper opinion concerning the establishment
of what was termed "an inner Cabinet" as generally reserved,
but it was unanimous in the conviction that the present u-
opeen hostilities and developments in the worla are of vital
importance to Japan and require the most careful treatment.
The close relation between the settlement of hostilities in
China and the developments in Europe was pointed out. Some
newspapers, such as the YOMIURI and the KOKUMIN, warned that
isolation for any nation is impossible today and predicted
that it may become necessary to reexemine Japan's policy of
non-involvement in the European war. These problem's, it was
believed, would inevitably be disoussed by the four-minister
conference.
The Imbassy was of the opinion that the establishment
of this compact, policy-making unit would not result in any
immediate changes in policy.

VI. GNERAL ECONOLIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITTONS.
(a) Rice Problem.*
From the various meagre press reports concerning the
Government's progrem for the purohasing of domestic rice
in the amount of 10,500,000 koku (1 koku = approximately
5 bushels) it was obvious that the progrem was meeting with
considerable

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.98)
1v
considerable difficulty. The linister of Agrigulture never-
theless urged the cooperation and assistance of various pre-
fectural governors and expressed confidence that the progrem
of purchasing domestic rice would be successfully completed
by the end of May. The Government undoubtedly had in mind
the necessity of acquiring these holdings in order to assure
the availability of a minimum amount of rice necessary for
the carry-oer period prior to the hervesting of this year's
crop. It was reported thet compulsory sile of rice had been
practioed in several areas. At the same time it became more
nearly apparent that the Government was being foroed to im-
port more foreign rice than had originally been anticipated.
The press was unanimous in its censure of the secrecy prac-
tioed by the Government concerning rice supplies and was
skeptical over the degree of success achieved by the Govern-
ment in meeting the shortage. The question remained a serious
politioal issue and rationing was undertaken in several small
ciroles and was suggested for the general Kwansai area around
Osake and Kobe. It was believed, however, that the problem
was still largely one of distribution, but there were in-
creasin: evidences that Japenese consumers as well as pro-
ducers of rice were not entirely confident of the Government's
ability to continue to meet the demand for rice. There was,
aocordingly, an intensification of the tendenoy to hoard this
essential commodity. Various factors, such as the probable
less than average quantity of fertilizer whioh would be avail-
able to farmers, the probable less than usual number of agri-
oultural laborers, and the small prosepot of inoreasing the
mount of equipment and materials available to farmors in-

probable that total production will be less than normal. It
is significant to noto that importation of rice was made neces-
sary by the unusual and unforesoen circumstances of the sovere
drought of last year. Foreign exchange funds set aside for
purohase of primary industrial materials were necessarily
diverted from their intended purposes, and that diversion made
moro diffioult international remittances end the purchase
of materials requirod for the Government's progrem of in-
dustrial expansion.
(b) Labor Problem.*
There have been for some time comments in the verna-
cular papers concerning a "shortage of labor" in Japan. It
was announced during the month of May that a spocial census
of laborers and toohnicians would be undertaken and would
be used as tho basis for "labor mobilization, strengthening
of the labor power of Japan, and improvement in the mobili-
zation of teohnicians". Tho most sorious shortago is to be
found in the ranks of skillod laborors and is not limited to
Japan alone but, from various reports, is prevalont through-
out the "yen-bloo area". In spite of the so-called labor
shortage, real wagos in Japan have not kopt paoe with in-
flationary trends and rising commodity pricos. It is to be
noted, however, that skilled workers are receiving oxcoptional-
ly high wages in the munitions and other heavy industries. In
many instances those laborers are paid salaries far above the
salaries ordinarily received by responsible government of-
ficials, store managers, end other workers.

I. RELATIONS VITH THE UJITED STATES
(a) The Grev-Arita Conversations.*
In analyzing the situation here early in the
nonth of June, it vas apparent to the Anbassador that
there existed in Japan a state of political turmoil of
unusual intensity. There vas one factor uniting all
elenents, and that was the desire to bring about an
early settlenent of the "China incident". Vice dif-
ferences of opinion were indicated, hovever, as to the
means considered cesirable to effect a settlermt and the
terms of the settlement itself. Some auvocated an under-
standing with Russia, looking to a partition of China
similar to that of Poland. The supporters of this line
of procedure also advocated seizure of the Netherlands
East Indies to forestall the taking over of those
islands by the Gernans. Those individuals vere not
seriously detracted by the almost certainty that the
United States vould undertake econonic reprisals against
such action by Japan. They discountec the possibility
of a var betveen the United States and Japan. There vas
a second. school of thought vhich vas fundanentally pro-
Gorman, and its hand vas being daily strenthened and its
prestige increased by the groving German success in
Europe

On June 28 the Minister for Foreign Affairs asked

Governnent had carefully studied the statement by the
Ambasse.or on June 19. In his opinion there were "no

to the fact that there exist hostilities in China. Mr.
Arita gave the Ambassador an "oral statement" and said
that except as modified by this oral statement the
Japanese Government agreed with the views of the Govern-
ment of the United States as orally presented by Mr.
Grew on June 19.
Mr. Arita agreed fully that every effort should
be made to minimize the unfortunate results of the
present European war. He stated that Japan's desires
were to see ell nations enjoy their proper places and
to see peece established and maintained throughout
the world. This enteiled, he said, respect for the prin-
ciples of nationel sovereignty, justice, law, end order.
It was considered necessary that each country eppreoiate
the other's position in the light of world changes and
actual conditions. Mr. Arita went on to assert that
although Japen had always advocated free movement of
both men and commodities, there existed immigration
restrictions and the control of markets anā of the flow

(b) Conditions at Shanghai.*
On June 4 the British Arb ssador informed Mr. Grew
that he was prepared to make representations to the
Japanese Foreign Office concerning terrorist activities
in Shanghai. The Ambassador proposed within the next
few days, as suitable occasion offered, to make an
oral and informal approach to the Foreign Office urging
the advisebility of doing away with unlawful armed
groups in areas under Jepanese control at Shanghai.
Early in June the vernacular press gave considerable
attention to a rumor from Shanghai to the effect that
the authorities in the French Concession there were ne-
gotiating for the transfer to the United States of the
responsibilities of policing the French Concession.
The pross also reported that strong representations were
to be made by the Jepanese Government to the French Go-
vornmont through the Fronch Ambassador at Tokyo. Inquiry
at the Fronch Embessy disclosed that the Foreign Office
had not communioated with the French Embassy conoerning
this quostion.

(c) United States Iierines - Japanese Gendarmes
inoident at Shanghai on July 7.*
On the morning of July 9 all the Japanese newspapers
prominently featured a DOMEI despatch from Shanghai stating
in summary as follows:
The

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
"The Commander of the Japanese Gendarmerie
looked upon the arrest of Japanese gendarmes and
their detention as an unfriendly act but was
willing to consider the matter closed following
the release of those gendarmes. The Commander
regretted that he had failed to notify the American
garrison in advance of the fact thet gendarmes were
being sent into the American defense sector. Pos-
sible negligence on the part of the Japanese in
failing to give notificetion does not excuse the
indescribably outrageous and insulting treatment
which the Jepanese gendarmes received following
their arrest. Investigation revealed that (1)
the American Harines struck the gendarmes with
the butts of their rifles and three or four were
struck in the face; (2) the Japenese were in-
sistently jabbed with rifles and were beaten
whenever they spoke or spat while in the truck
en route to American Marine headquerters; (3)
the gendarmes were forced to sit in the rifle
practice yard and were threatened with loaded
rifles as though they were about to be executed."
In addition to the above DOrEI report practically all
newspepers carried special despatches from individual
Shanghai correspondents emphasizing the "outrageous and
insulting" treatment accorded the Japanese gendarmes even
after their identity was known to the American Marines. It
was reported that the Japanese authorities viewed the
situation with great seriousness for the reason that those
"outrageous acts" had been cormitted intentionelly and
vithout provocetion.
In con oraity vith the Department's instructions, the
nbassy on July 13 made an approach to the Foreign Office
concerning this incident and stated that the Secretary of
State had in recent press conferences made clear his belief
that the situation could be adjusted locally on a basis of
mutual prudence and reasonableness. It was pointed out,
however, that the effecting of such a local settlement was
rendered more difficult as a result of numerous statements
sensational in character whioh had reportedly been made by
various Japanese authorities at Shanghai and had been pro-
minently reported in Japanese newspapers there. It was
suggested

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
suggested that it would probcbly be helpful if the Japanese
Government could issue appropriate instructions to Japanese
officials at Shanghai to use their best efforts to discou-
rage the issuance or publication of sensational statements.
The Foreign Ofrice was informea that American officials at
Shanghai were being inst ucted cerefully to refrain from
statements which might be the pretext for further sensation-
alizing of the incident. The Foreign Office in reply said
that the Japanese Government was in accord with this view
but that it would be unnecessary to issue the instructions
suggested, since the Japanese Consul General et Shanghai hed
already repoted by telegreph that he had taken necessary
steps. The Embassy was also informed that the Foreign
Office had instructed the Jepanese Cousul General to use
his best efforts to effect a local settlement between the
militery cormanders concerned.
There were no further sensationel reports in the press
during July, nor was the matter discussed further by the
Embessy with the Foreign Office here.

(g) Reaction in Japan to the reported inclusion of
petroleum, petroleum products, end scrap metal
in the list of cormodities for licensed export.*
Toward the end of July there were prominently featured
in the Japanese press despatches from the United States re-
porting that additional commodities, including petroleum and
sorap iron, were to be placed under a system of licensed
exports by the American Government. The press reported on
July 27 that the Foreign Office spokesman in commenting on
these press despatches stated that it was desirable to avoid
definite comment pending the receipt of an official report to
the effect thet licenses for the restricted conmodities had
been refused for export to Japan. He did say, however, that
the inclusion in the list of restricted commodities of pe-
troleum and scrap netal, of which the United States has an
excess, required the fullest study concerning the motives
of the American Government in taking such action. The spokes-
man made the assumption that the United States had also care-
fully considered the probable effects of the neasures on the
international situation. Some newspapers considered this
measure as reflective of American animosity against Japan and
a reprisal for the closing of the Burma route. The general
reation in the press.was, however, not to expect drastic and
serious reduction in petroleum exports to Japcan, and the
assertion was made that while prohibition of the export of
sorap metals would adversely affect the Japanese steel in-
dustry, the large manufacturers had already taken adequate
measures to carry out Japen's industrial program.

reperoussions in Jepen, end a clarificction of Japanese policy

On July 8 the British Ambassador left with the inister

of his Government on this question. The memorandum stated
that with regard to Eong Kong the transit of munitions had
been prohibited since January l939 and no war materials of any

question of the Burma route, the British Ambassador's memo-
randum stated that it was true that a small quantity of arms

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Cabinet.*
In analyzing the domestic political situation on July
2, the Ambassador reported to the Department that there
were many indications that the efforts to overthrow the
Cabinet and to bring about a more drastic foreign policy
were coming to a head. The consensus of opinion at that
time was that the chenges might be expected soon after the
return of the Emperor of "Manchukuo" to his country on July
7 and that the new Cabinet would be formed by Prince Konoye.
The critical political situation had been brought into sharp
focus by the circumstances attending the radio address of
the Minister for Foreign Affairs on June 29. The report
which had appeared in all newspapers the previous morning
that an official declaration of a policy of cooperation with
Germany and Italy would be forthcomin; hed not had the appro-
val or knowledge of the Prime Linister. The general impres-
sion given by the ambiguous radio address of the iinister for
Foreign Affairs wes that while he might have favored a strong
foreign policy he was circumscribed by a weak Army and Navy.
On July 2 the vernacular press stated briefly and in
guarded terms that the War Minister was carefully investiga-
ting the "ciroumstances leading up to the radio address of the
Minister

of the Cabinet.
On July 16 the entire Cabinet resigned. The following
day the vernacular press in reporting the resignetion of the
Yonai Cabinet was virtually unanimous in stating that poli-
tical change was necessary at that time in order to (a)
strengthen national unity through the "new political struc-
ture" and (b) "revitalize and strengthen" Japan's foreign
polioy. The resignation of the Cabinet was said to heve
grown out of a graduelly widening divergence of opinion be-
tween the "Army and the Cabinet." The press agreed in con-
sidoring Prince Konoye as the only candidate likely to re-
ceive the Imperial Command to form a new Governnent. This
prediction was realized on July 17 when the Emperor commanded

Prince Konoye to undertake the responsibility of forming 8

On July 18 the press reported thet rince Konoye had
had a long conversation with the former Var and Navy Mini-
sters and had exchanged views vith them. It was said that
the new Premier's policy would be to select the War, Nevy and
Foreign Ministers first and in a conference with them to
decide upon and obtain ogreement to Jepan's high policy
before proceeding with the selection of other ministers and
important Government officials. uestions to be decided at
this conference were said to be revision of Japan's policy of
non-involvement in the European hostilities, strengthening
the Rome-Tokyo-Berlin axis and other fundanental changes in
Japan's foreign policy as well as inportant domestic poli-
cies. The pres in general emphasized that the new Cabinet
should be in harmony with nilitary circles and should there-
fore beoome a strong enduring Cabinet.
Vernacular newspapers reported during the early days of
the Cabinet formation that Prince Konoye desired to realize
close cooperation and understanding between the civil Govern-
ment and the Army authorities. It was suggested that there
would probably be revived the liaison conferences and con-
feronces before the Throne which hed been helå frequently
during the previous administration of Prince Konoye. A meet-
ing of the four inisters, the Prenier, General Tojo, War
Minister, Admiral Yoshida, Navy Linister, and r. Matsuoka,
Foreign Minister, which was characterized as the "Supreme
National Defense Council" was held from four o'clock on July
19.
In an analysis of the Cabinet chenge the Ambassador re-
ported to the Department that the s leotion of Prince Konoye
as

as the new Prime Minister depended upon the question vhether
the hostilities in China coula be expected to be brought to
an end within the reasonably neer future. Prince Konoye's
selection was, therefore, in the Embassy's opinion, a clear
indication that Jepanese official circles were becoming more
optinistic concerning the prospects of bringing the China
conflict to an end. Such optimism was probably derived in a
certein measure from the events in Europe. The precise
reasons for the resignation of the Yonai Cabinet were not at
that time fully exposed. Te Japanese public, although it was
not fully aware of the fact thet ∴dmiral Yonai had been per-
sistently skeptical over the benefits of close association
with Germany and Italy nor of his lack of enthusiesm for the
economic new order in East Asia, sensed a lack of vigor on
the part of the Yonai Cabinet in approaching problems direc-
tly and indirectly related to the Cli?: incident. At the same
time the speed of events in Europe seem to impel Japan toward
closer reletions with Germany and Italy. It is not to be over-
looked that the Japanese people were eager after a decade of
almost complete isolation to grasp the hands of friendly
nations which had demonstreted their military strength.
The new C inet was on the whole believed to be conserva-
tive with the exceptions of . Hoshino, Iinister without
Portfolio and Director of the Planning Bo rd, Ir. Kazami,
Minister of Justice, and possibly ir. Matsuoka, inister for
Foreign Affairs. It was not expected that there would be any
abrupt or drastic change in foreign policy in the immediate
future. It was believed that economio considerations would
preelude any definitive and irresponsible action by Japan at
that time although the general trend was certainly toward
closer relations with the axis Powers. Concrete action was

and at the sam: time it was not expected that Japan would

VI. GEIERAL FINANCIAL AND CONONIC CONDITIONS*
During the month of July cautiousness pervaded business,
financial and industrial circles. This general atmosphere
was caused by the feeling of unoertainty with regard to poli-
tical developments. Early in the month it was generally
expected that the Cabinet could probably not meintain its
position. Following the appointment of Premier Konoye there
remained considerable hesitation in economic circles pending
a clarification of the new Government's policies. General
business conditions were lergely at a standstill. All share
prices on the various exchanges in Japan showed a declining
tendency. Bank note circulation was slightly contracted during
the month, and the absorption of national bonds was imper-
ceptibly reduced.
Legislation looking to the prohibition of the manufacture
and sale of luxury articles brought about slight increase in
the mumber of bankruptoies during the month of July. Business
circles were of the opinion that this legislation was the fore-
runner of more intensive regulation of every article con-
sidered non-essential. Full enforcement of these laws would
bring
Prepared with the Assistance of the office of the Commsroial
Attaché.

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.99)
-28-
bring about a change in employment of great numbers of
laborers end manufacturers. The menufacture of luury articles
was prohibited as of July 7 and sale of those erticles would
be prohibited as from Octcber 7th. A serious drivo to force
the export of luxury articles might be expected.
As has already been stated the general apprehension in
economic circles was shared by industrialists. Industrial
activity, however, continued on a fairly high level and with
the improvement in water supplies there was available more
hydroelectric power. Coal distribution was accelerated. Iron
and steel share prices declined following the announcenent
that scrap iron and steel would be placed under a licensing
system.for export by the United States.
Total exports from the Japanese Empire for the month of
July were Yen 358,260,000 while total imports for the Jcpa-
nese Empire were Yen 281,806,000.
Total exports for Jepan Proper totalled Yen 329,302,000,
while total imports for Japan Proper amounted to Yen
256,944,000.
The Embassy has up to the present been unable to obtain
a breakdown of these figures which would indicate the rela-
tive amounts of exports and imports to and from non yen-bloc
countries.
VII. ACTIVITIES OF ANERICAN COMERCIAL IINTERESTS.*
Early in July it was brought to the Anbassador's at-
tention that the firm of Andrews and George, long-established
in Japan as the sole agent for various American machine tool
manufactures, was being subjected to increasing pressure from
certain elements in the Ministry of Commerce and emong
Japanese
*Psemered with the assistance of the office of the Commercial
attaoh6.

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.99)
-29-
Japanese business circles to force the sale of the Company to
the Japanese firm, Sanyo Shokai, vhich is entirely German-
controlled. Details of the terms of sale had not been worked
out but it was indicated that the American branch of Andrews
and George and Company in New York voulá continue to function
and that the Jepenese Company would deal only with Andrev's
and George in New York. This would in effect result in the
control by Germen interests of the sale of American mechine
tools in Japan.
On July 16 the Ministry of Commerce end Industry issued
a written order to Andrews and George for the sale of its
company to interests here in Jepan. This order provided
for more than fifty per cent Jepanese control of the Company
and in a separate paragraph stipulated that 40 percent or
less of the shares were to be sold to "X", presumebly the
German company. The order also required that "part of the
company's foreign currency be used as directed by the lachine
Bureau of the Department of Commerce and Industry.
A later conversation with Mr. R. . Andrews of Andrews
and George indicated that the order from the Iinister of
Commerce and Industry had been issued by a minor official
and that officials of the Finance Ministry were indignant at
the treatment being given the American firm and had in-
dicated that they would give Andrews and George active as-
sistance in this question. In view of these ciroumstances
it was apparent that the case had not developed to the point
of involving questions of Japanese law or policy. The Embassy
therefore withheld the taking of any action.
This case, however, was one of the more extrome examples
of pressure being brought to bear on American firms in Japen
through

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.97)
-30-
through Japanese laws and regulations requiring import and
exohange permits. The Embassy continued to follow the general
question carefully throughout the month of July.

Tokyo's "Roport of Conditions in Japan
Auring the month of August 1940" (despatch
no. 5054 dated Ootober 8, 1940)..
Most of the material of interest in
this report has already been received in
the Division in previous telegrams and des-
patches, reference to which is made in
various footnotes to the report. General
divisions end subjects of the report are
indicated in the *Index and Summary"
following the title page.
There is no indication in the report
that the following information ("General
Financial and Economic Conditions") has been
previously received in the Division.
During August Japanese business circles
were frankly apprehensive with regard to the
future cour se of the new Government's control
over business. The stock market was weak.
The rate of national bond absorption
was characterized as "poor"--71 percent as
compared with 104.5 percent during July.
The

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.98)
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2
The average daily note issue of the
Bank of Japan reached a new high.
Wholesale and retail prices advanced
sharply over the corresponding figures for
the preceding year.
At the month end the Prime Minister
announced more rigid controls of trade in
the yen bloo area.
For the month there was a foreign
trade export balance of ¥31,399,000, as
compared with an export balance of
¥76,694,000 in July.

(e) Reaction in Japan to the American Defense
Program of the Western Hemisphere.*
There was considerable editorial commont following the
conclusion of the joint defense agreement between the United
States and Canada. The ASAHI of August 20 similar to other
vernacular newspapers stated that the action was a logical
stop and not unexpected but urged that whilo the agreement
had no direct or immediate relationship with Japan, it was
nevertheless necessery for Japan to watch carefully tho
diplomatic activities of the United States and of Soviet
Russia in the drastic and rapidly changing world conditions.
The HocHI asserted that the agreemont affected not only
the Atlantic but also the Pacific, the West Pacific and the

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
1. Arrest of British subjects iu Tapen ani Japanese
subjects in British Empire.*
On August 3 all the vernacular newspapers in Tokyo
issued extra editions reporting the arrest of prominent
Japanese in London. This action was considered by the press
to be entirely retaliatory for the arrest of British subjects
in Japan. This original announcement was followed by more
elaborate comment in the press curing the following few days.
The Japanese arrested were said to be prominent businessmen
above any possible suspicion of espionage. Some journals
went so far as to characterize the Blitish acticn as barberous
and illegal. Fear was expressed conccrning the possibility
394.4121
of a grave crisis in Anglo-Japanese rolations shouid the
question be not promptly settled, and some newspapers even
suggested the nocessity of taking positive measures against
Great Britain. In spito of these instances of somewhat violent
language the goneral undortone of the press was, in fact,
weak. On August 5 the Embassy was informed that three pro-
minent British citizens who had been arrestod in Kobe were
allowed to return home. Owing to the fact that some of the
British nationals arrested in Japan might have been in a
position to inform the British authorities concerning actual

(2) Withdrawal of British military garrisons at
Shanghai, Peiping and Tiontsin.*
On August 10 the British Embassy delivered to the
Foreign Office a notification that British troops which had
been maintained in China at Shanghai, Peiping and Tientsin
would be withdrawn. The British Government reserved all its
treaty rights under the Poiping Protocol of September 7, 190l.
The reaction in the vernacular press following the announce-
ment of the British Government's intention to withdraw its
forces in China was distinctly unfavorable to Great Britain.
It was characterized as a futile gesturo in an attempt to
improve Anglo-Japanese relations end simultanoously to

that Great Britain couli not expect to improve its rela-

tively with Japan. The press was also very suspicious con-
cerning the United States and stated that some prior under-
standing between the United States and Great Britain had
probably been reached.
On August 19 the vernacular press stated that as it
had previously predicted and subsequent events had establi-
shed, the British withdrawal of troops from China was only
an expedient measure designed to ameliorate anti-British
sentiment in Japan and to ease the strain of Anglo-Jepanese
relations. Subsequent events, it was reported, had verified
the accuracy of previously expressed suspicion that a secret
understanding had been reached between the United States and
Great Britain. The vernacular press went on to assert that
the responsibility for the preservation of peace and order
at Shanghai lay with the Japanese forces there and that there
was no legal foundation of the stationing of foreign troops
at Shanghai. It was said that the Japaneso forces intended
to take o ver the general defense of the Internetional Settle-
ment only until such time as the "National Government" at
Nanking wes able to perform those functions.

(b) The Netherlands East Indies.*
On August 3 it was reported in the press that General
Koiso, former Minister of Overseas Affairs in the first
Konoye Cabinet (1937-1938) had been offered the position
of official delegate from the J apanese Government to the
Netherlands East Indies and that he would be given broad
powers and the highest rank. The purpose of General Koiso's
mission was said to be to establish the closest political
and economic relations between the two areas on the basis
of mutual interest and position within the sphere of greater
East Asia. At the same time certain newspapers reported
that while en route to Tokyo General Koiso had stated, inter
alia, Japan China and Manchukuo" alone were insufficient for
the construction of an economic zone in Greater East Asia;
that it was necessary to include the South Seas area; that
from a moral viewpoint the freeing of the Oriental races was
necessary and was destined or realization; that the Nether-
lands Indies had long been exploited as a colony and had been
subjected to "administrative pressure". General Koiso en-
visaged the possibility of friction with the United States
in the oxecution of this program and said that Japan's re-
solute determination was required.

(f) China.
1. Military Operations in China.
Japanese military operations in China during the month
of August, with the exception of aerial cperations, were on
the whole limited to what were characterized as "mopping up"
expeditions. More important fighting took place in Central
Hopei and Eastern Shansi in which areas well coordinated and
quite effective raids on railways were conducted by the Chinese
Eighth Route Army. According to Japanese announcements "mopping
up" operations against those units of the Eighth Route Army
793.94
countinued throughout the month of August.
In northeastern Anhwei Province the Japanese Army also
carried out operations against Chinese guerilla forces opera-
ting in that general district.
Naval landing parties during the month of August carried
out a series of minor raids along the South Chino Coast.
These Japanese marine forces did not attempt to occupy any
territory but merely landed and in raids destroyed some supplies
and then withdrew.
Both the Japanese Army and Navy air arms continued their
long: distanco raids on Chungking and other points in Szechuan
Province. In addition to these long-range aorial bombing
oporations, the Army air forccs bombed the communicetions
lines in Kwangsi Province.

of the step taken and there were only a few veiled and in-
definite queries as to the advisability of the measure.

(c) New National Structure.*
On August 28 Prince Konoye issued a statement prior to
the opening of the first session of the Preparatory Com-
mittoe on the establishment of the new structure. This
statement in surmary was as follows:
"If Japan is to bring the China Affair to a
successful conclusion and to tako an active part
in the establishmont of a new world order Japen
must perfect a national defense structure of the
highest degree. The basis of such a structure is
c powerful internal structure. This is indeed a
netionel demand. It is not a domend of a tomporcry
chercoter but of a permanent one. Whether or not
Jepan can ostablish such n strong nationcl structure
will decide the very riso or fall of the netiona.
Among the items to be considered (are) harmonious
cooperation between the High Command and the administra-
tive branch of the Government, the consolidation of
State mechanisms and the heightening of efficiency and
the establishment of a new Parliamentary structure as
an organ for assisting the Throne. The aim of the
new national structure is to unite the total energies
of the state. The organization of the nation must
therefore extend to the economic and cultural spheres.
There must be a solid nation-wide structure in which
all component parts are organized vertically, and
they are also bound together horizontally. A definite
national movement is necessary for the successful
realization of this national structure. Such a
movement
*mbassy's tolograms Nos. 759, August 28, 7 p.m.; 780
August 28, 8 p.m. Dospatch No. 4946, Soptombor 5, 1940.

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
movement should spontaneously spring from the people
themselves. If it is planned or guided by the Govern-
ment, or it is given anadministrative structure, it
may hinder the spontaneous manifestation of the people's
energies. The present circumstances, however, do not
allow us to rely only cn the spontaneous develop-
ment of such a movemont. Mercover, agitations from
bolow, aro liable to dcgencrato into factionel strifes
and fail to expand into a roally national movemont. The
Govornment have thus found it nocossary to take posi-
tive steps for fostering and directing this movemont.
This movement is a common undertaking of both the
Government and the people: it is a nation-wide move-
mont to assist the Throne. The new national structure
movement aims at superseding the old party politics
postulated upon liberalism. When such a movement
is led by tho Government itself it cannot in any
sense be a party movement. Noither can it be allowed
to tako tho form of a single party system. No matter
what brilliant results such a system may have reaped
in other lands, it is not acceptable in Japan because
it is contrary to the basic principle of our national
polity of 'One Sovereign over all'. In Japan it
is the privilege of all His Imperial Majesty's
subjects to assist the Throne and that privilege
cannot be monopolized by the power of either a single
individual or a single party. If there should arise
a difference of opinion concerning the assistance
to be offered, the final decision would rest with the
Throne. And once an Imperial decision has been
given, all subjects of the Throne should unite in
obeying His Majesty's word. That is the true essence
of Japanese polity."
Although the sessions of the Preparatory Committee
began there were no significant developments nor was there
any clarification of the "new national structure" during
the month under review.

During the month of August business and industrial
circles in Japan were frankly apprehensive regarding the
futuro course of Government control over their activities
and the possible involvcment with third Powers over the
question of Japanese entrance into French Indo-China. The
share market was generally weak and leading speculative
issues reached their lowest price levels at the end of the
month since the outbreak of the European hostilities.
On August 29 Prime Minister Konoye announced more
rigid price control for the trado in the yen-bloc area, the
extonsion of stricter official control over numerous industries
and tho functions of various trade organizations and businoss
concerns. Immediately after this Government announcement,
shares of the Tokyo Stock Exchange, the so-called "barometer"
of the market, dropped from Yon l26 to a record low of Yen
109.

X. MISC ELLANEOUS.
On August 24 the Ambassador was informed by a foreign
bishcp of the Protestant Episcopal Church that an under-
standing had been reached between the gendarmerie and the
senior Japanese Bishop, Naide, under which in order to fore-
stall pressure against the church no foreigners would there-
after be allowed to have charge of a diocese or a church in
Jepan and all financial assistence from abroad would be
refused.
This movement ageinst foreign missioncries, it was
believed, resulted from or attitude of the Japanese mil-
1tery authoritios that (a) the Christian Church is dissemina-
ting propagandε fevorable to the United States and Great
Britain which dissemination is contrary to netionel policy
and inimical to Japan, (b) finencial support from abroad
is likely to result in the control hy foroign interest of
Japanese

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.99)
-37-
Japenese clergymen receiving such support and (c) all
foreign missionaries are potential spies.
This movement began early in August with the arrest
of the leaders of the Salvation Army. It was reported that
the Methodist Church would be next to be "purged" by the
gendarmerie. Leading missionaries were of the opinion that
this movement would mark the end of missionary activities
in Jap an.
On August 30 there was reported the meeting of 50
representatives of various Christian organizations in
Japan under the chairmanship of Mr. Abe, President of the
Federation of Christian Churches in Japan. The meeting was
called for the purpose of adopting a common policy for
Christian churchos in Japan which would be conformable to
the "new netional structure." It wes reported that
offorts would be made to have the various Christian sots
amalgamate into one organization and that all foreign
assistance and influence would be eliminated from Chris-
tien andeavors. The meeting adjourned without achieving by
concrete results but wes scheduled to be reconvened on
September 2 for further discussions of this question as well
as of Christian missionary activities in Mcnchukuo" end
China.

American Marines and Japanese gendarmes.*

Americen Government, after careful review of the correspondencc
between the Americen end Japanese militery authoritios at

to expross an opinion with regard to tho attitude of the

(e) Reaction in Japan to the American Defense
Program of the Western Hemisphere.*
There was considerable editorial commont following the
conclusion of the joint defense agreement between the United
States and Canada. The ASAHI of August 20 similar to other
vernacular newspapers stated that the action was a logicel
stop and not unexpected but urged that while the agreement
had no direct or immediate relationship with Japan, it was
nevertheless necessery for Japan to watch carefully tho
diplomatic activities of the United States and of Soviet
Russia in the drastic and rapidly changing world conditions.
The HochI assorted that the agreemont affected not only

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britain.
1. Arrest of British subjects in Tapen ana Japatese
subjects in British Empire.*
On August 3 all the vernacular newspapers in Tokyo
issued extra editions reporting the arrest of prominent
Japanese in London. This action was considered by the press
to be entirely retaliatory for the arrest of British subjects
in Japan. This original announcement was followed by more
elaborate comment in the press curing the following few days.
The Japanese arrested were said to be prominent businessmen
above any possible suspicion of espionage. Some journals
went so far as to characterize th? British action as barbarous
and illegal. Fear was expressed conccrning the possibility
of a grave crisis in Anglo-Japanese rulations should the
question be not promptly settled, ana some newspepers even
suggested the nocessity of taking positive measures against
Great Britain. In spito of these instances of somewhat violent
language the goneral undortone of the press was, in fact,
weak. On August 5 the Embassy was informed that three pro-
minent British citizens who had beon arrestod in Kobe were
allowed to roturn home. Owing to the fact that some of the
British nationals arrested in Japan might have boen in a
position to inform the British authorities concerning actual
transshipments

(2) Withdrawal of British militery garrisons at
Shanghai, Peiping and Tientsin.*
On August 10 tho British Embassy delivered to the
Foreign Office a notification that British troops which had
been maintained in China at Shanghai, Peiping and Tientsin
would be withdrawn. The British Government resorved all its
treaty rights under the Poiping Protocol of September 7, 1901.
The reaction in the vernacular press following the announce-
ment of the British Government's intention to withdraw its
forces in China was distinctly unfavorable to Great Britain.
It was characterizcd as a futile gesture in an attempt to
improve Anglo-Japanese rolations end simultanoously to
strengthen

that Great Britain couli not expect to improve its rela-

cerning the United States end stated that some prior under-
standing between the United States and Great Britain had
probably been reached.
On August 19 the vernacular press stated that as it
had previously predicted and subsequent events had establi-
shed, the British withdrawal of troops from China was only
an expedient measure designed to ameliorate anti-British
sentiment in Japan and to ease the strain of Anglo-Jepanese
relations. Subsequent events, it was reported, had verified
the accuracy of previously expressed suspicion that a secret
understanding had been reached between the United States and
Great Britain. The vernacular press went on to assert that
the responsibility for the preservation of peace and order
at Shanghai lay with the Japanese forces there end that there
was no legal foundation of the stationing of foreign troops
at Shanghai. It was seid that the Japaneso forces intended
to take o ver the general defense of the International Settle-

Nanking was able to perform those functions.

(b) The Netherlands East Indies.*
On August 3 it was reported in the press that General
Koiso, former Minister of Overseas Affairs in the first
Konoye Cabinet (1937-1938) had been offered the position
of official delegate from the J apanese Government to the
Netherlands East Indies and that he would be given broad
powers and the highest rank. The purpose of General Koiso's
mission was said to be to establish the closest political
and economic relations between the two areas on the basis
of mutual interest and position within the sphere of greater
East Asia. At the same time certain newspapers reported
that while en route to Tokyo General Koiso had stated, inter
alia, Japan China and Manchukuo" alone were insufficient for
the construction of an economic zone in Greater East Asia;
that it was necessary to include the South Seas area; that
from a moral viewpoint the freeing of the Oriental races was
necessary and was destined or realization; that the Nether-
lands Indies had long been exploited as a colony and had been
subjected to "administrative pressure". General Koiso en-
visaged the possibility of friction with the United States
in the oxecution of this program and said that Japan's re-
solute determination was required.
On August 5 the vernacular press gave considerable

(f) China.
1. Military Operations in China.
Japanese military operations in China during the month
of August, with the exception of aerial operations, were on
the whole limited to what were cheracterized as "mopping up"
expeditions. More important fighting took place in Central
Hopei and Eastern Shansi in which areas well coordinated and
quite effective raids on railways vwere conducted by the Chinese
Eighth Route Army. According to Japanese announcements "mopping
up" operations against those units of the Eighth Route Army
countinued throughout the month of August.
In northeastern Anhwei Province the Japanese Army also
carried out operations against Chinese guerilla forces opera-
ting in that general district.
Navel landing parties during the month of August carried
out a series of minor raids along the South China Coast.
These Japanese marine forces did not attempt to occupy any
territory but merely landed and in raids destroyed some supplies
and then withdrew.
Both the Japanese Army and Navy air arms continued their
long: distanco raids on Chungking and other points in Szechuan
Province. In addition to these long-range aerial bombing
oporations, thc Army air forccs bombed the communicetions
lines in Kwangsi Province.

(c) New National Structure.*
On August 28 Prince Konoye issued a statement prior to
the opening of the first session of the Preparatory Com-
mittee on the establishment of the new structure. This
statement in surmary was as follows:
"If Japan is to bring the China Affair to a
successful conclusion and to tako an active part
in the establishmont of a new world order Japan
must perfect a national defense structure of the
highest degree. The basis of such a structure is
ε powerful internal structure. This is indeed a
netionel demand. It is not a domend of a tomporcry
charccter but of a permanent one. Whether or not
Jepan can ostablish such n strong national structure
will decide the very rise or fall of the netiona.
Among the items to be considered (are) harmonious
cooperation between the High Command and the administra-
tive branch of the Government, the consolidation of
State mechanisms and the heightening of efficiency and
the establishment of a new Parliamentary structure as
an organ for assisting the Throne. The aim of the
new national structure is to unite the total energies
of the state. The organization of the nation must
therefore extend to the economic and cultural spheres.
There must be a solid nation-wide structure in which
all component parts are organized vertically, and
they are also bound together horizontally. A definite
national movement is necessary for the successful
realization of this national structure. Such a
movement
*mbassy's telograms Nos. 759, August 28, 7 p.m.; 780,
August 28, 8 p.m. Dospatch No. 4946, Soptombor 5, 1940.

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.99)
-32-
movement should spontaneously spring from the people
themselves. If it is planned or guided by the Govern-
ment, or it is given anedministrative structure, it
may hinder the spontaneous manifestation of the people's
energies. The present circumstances, however, do not
allow us to rely only on the spontaneous develop-
ment of such a movemont. Icrcover, agitations from
bolow, are liable to dcgenorate into factionel strifos
and fail to expand into a really national movemont. The
Govornment have thus found it nocessary to take posi-
tive steps for fostering and directing this movemont.
This movement is a common undertaking of both the
Government and the people: it is a nation-wide move-
mont to assist the Throne. The new national structure
movement aims at superseding the old party politics
postulated upon liberalism. When such a movement
is led by tho Government itself it cannot in any
sense be a party movement. Noither can it be allowed
to tako tho form of a single party system. No matter
what brilliant results such a system may have reaped
in other lands, it is not acceptable in Japen because
it is contrary to the basic principle of our national
polity of 'One Sovereign over all'. In Japan it
is the privilege of all His Imperial Majesty's
subjects to assist the Throne and that privilege
cannot be monopolized by the power of either a single
individual or a single party. If there should arise
a difference of opinion cyncerning the assistance
to be offered, the final decision would rest with the
Throne. And once an Imperial decision has been
given, ell subjects of the Throne should unite in
obeying His Majesty's word. That is the true essence
of Japanese polity."
Although the sessions of the Preparatory Committee
began there were no significant developments nor was there
any clarification of the "new national structure" during
the month under review.

X. MISCELLANEOUS.
On August 24 the Ambassador was informed by a foreign
bishop of the Protestant Episcopal Church that an under-
standing had been reached between the gendarmerie and the
senior Japanese Bishop, Naide, under which in order to fore-
stall pressure against the church no foreigners would there-
after be allowed to have charge of a diocese or a church in
Jepan and all financial assistence from abroad would be
refused.
This movement against foreign missioneries, it was
believed, resulted from ar attitude of the Japanese mil-
itery authoritios that (c) the Christian Church is dissemina-
ting propagandε fevorable to the United States and Great
Britain which dissemination is contrary to netionel policy
and inimical to Japan, (b) finencial support from abroad
is likely to result in the control hy foreign interest of
Japanese

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.99)
-37-
Japanese clergymen receiving such support and (c) all
foreign missionaries are potential spies.
This movement began early in August with the arrest
of the leaders of the Salvation Army. It was reported that
the Methodist Church would be next to be "purged" by the
gendarmerie. Leading missionaries were of the opinion that
this movement would mark the end of missionary activities
in Jap an.
On August 30 there was reported the meeting of 50
representatives of verious Christian organizations in
Japan under the chairmanship of Mr. Abe, President of the
Federation of Christian Churches in Japan. The mecting was
called for the purpose of adopting a common policy for
Christian churchcs in Japan which would be conformeble to
the "new national structure." It was reported that
offorts would be made to have the various Christian sots
amalgamate into one organization and that all foreign
assistance and influence would be eliminated from Chris-
tien endeavors. The meeting adjourned without achieving by
concrete results but wes scheduled to be reconvened on
September 2 for further discussions of this question as well
as of Christian missionary activities in Manchukuo" end
China.

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) Great Britein.
1. Arrest of British subjects jn Tapen duu Japauese
subjects in British Empire.*
On August 3 all the vernacular newspapers in Tokyo
issued extra editions reporting the arrest of prominent
Japanese in London. This action was considered by the press
to be entirely retaliatory for the arrest of British subjects
in Japan. This original announcement was followed by more
elaborate comment in the press curing the following few days.
The Japanese arrested were said to be prominent businessmen
above any possible suspicion of espionage. Some journals
went so far as to characterize the Biitish action as barbarous
and illegal. Fear was expressed corccrning the possibility
of a grave crisis in Anglo-Japanese rolations should the
question be not promptly settled, and some newspapers even
suggested the nocessity of taking positive measures against
Great Britain. In spito of those instances of somewhat violent
language the goneral undortone of the press was, in fact,
weak. On August 5 the Embassy was informed that three pro-
minent British citizons who had been arrestod in Kobe were
allowed to roturn home. Owing to the fact that some of the
British nationals arrested in Japan might have been in a
position to inform the British authorities concerning actual

treaty richts under the Poiping Protocol of September 7, 1901.
The reaction in the vernacular press following the announce-
ment of the British Government's intention to withdraw its
forces in Chinc was distinctly unfavorable to Great Britain.
It was characturizcd as a futile gesture in an attempt to
improve Anglo-Japanese rolations end simultanoously to

(b) The Netherlands East Indies.*
On August 3 it was reported in the press that General
Koiso, former Minister of Overseas Affairs in the first
Konoye Cabinet (1937-1938) had been offered the position
of official delegate from the J apanese Government to the
Netherlands East Indies and that he would be given broad
powers and the highest rank. The purpose of General Koiso's
mission was said to be to establish the closest political
and economic relations between the two areas on the basis
of mutual interest and position within the sphere of greater
East Asia. At the same time certain newspapers reported
that while en route to Tokyo General Koiso had stated, inter
alia, Japan China and Manchukuo" alone were insufficient for
the construction of an economic zone in Greater East Asia;
that it wes necessary to include the South Seas area; that
from a moral viewpoint the freeing of the Oriental races was
necessary and was destined or realization; that the Nether-
lands Indies had long been exploited as a colony and had been
subjected to "administrative pressure". General Koiso en-
visaged the possibility of friction with the United States
in the execution of this program and said that Japan's re-
solute determination was required.

(f) China.
1. Military Operations in Chine.
Japanese military operations in China during the month
of August, with the exception of aerial operations, were on
the whole limited to what were cheracterized as "mopping up"
expeditions. More important fighting took place in Central
Hopei and Eastern Shansi in which areas well coordinated and
quite effective raids on railways vere conducted by the Chinese
Eighth Route Army. According to Japanese announcements "mopping
up" operations against those units of the Eighth Route Army
countinued throughout the month of August.
In northeastern Anhwei Province the Japanese Army also
carried out operations against Chinese guerilla forces opera-
ting in that general district.
Navel landing parties during the month of August carried
out a series of minor raids along the South China Coast.
These Japanese marine forces did not attempt to occupy any
territory but merely landed and in raids destroyed some supplies
and then withdrew.
Both thc Japancse Army and Navy air arms continued their
long: distanco raids on Chungking and other points in Szechuan
Province. In addition to those long-range aorial bombing
oporations, tho Army air forccs bombed the communicstions
lines in Kwangsi Province.

(b) Changes in Foreign Office and Foreign
Service Personnel.*
Early in August, the appointment of Mr. Chuichi Ohashi,
formerly Counselor to tho Government of "Manchukuo" as Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs was announced. On August 23
there appeared in the pross announcements of the recall of
the great majority of Japanese Ambassadors, Ministers, and
many other high-ranking Jepanese Foreign Service officials.
The subject was sensationally treated by the press and was
reported as "an unprecedented and drastic shakeup in Foreign
Service personnel". There was, however, no putright criticism
of the step taken and there were only a few veiled and in-
definite queries as to the advisability of the measure.
